## 🏗️ Cloud Deployment Models

| Deployment Model | Description |
|----|----|
| Public Cloud | Shared infrastructure managed by providers (AWS, Azure, GCP). |
| Private Cloud | Organization-owned cloud infrastructure. |
| Hybrid Cloud | Mix of public + private cloud. |
| Community Cloud | Shared by similar organizations (e.g., government agencies). |

## ☁️ Core Cloud Concepts

### 📈 Elasticity

Elasticity is the ability of a cloud environment to **automatically
scale resources up or down** in response to demand - without manual
intervention. This is one of the defining characteristics of cloud
computing and a direct advantage over on-premises infrastructure.

- A web server hosting a retail site can automatically spin up 20
  additional VM instances during a holiday sale and scale back down to 2
  instances when traffic drops - paying only for what was used.

- On-premises hardware cannot do this. You buy for peak capacity, and
  those resources sit idle the rest of the time.

**Elasticity vs. Scalability:** These terms appear together on the exam.
*Scalability* is the ability to grow capacity - it can be manual.
*Elasticity* specifically means **automatic**, on-demand scaling. All
elastic systems are scalable; not all scalable systems are elastic.

### 🔁 High Availability (HA)

High Availability refers to a system or service designed to remain
**operational with minimal downtime**, even when individual components
fail. It is measured as a percentage of uptime over a given period.

| Availability | Downtime per Year | Common Name |
|--------------|-------------------|-------------|
| 99%          | ~87.6 hours       | Two nines   |
| 99.9%        | ~8.76 hours       | Three nines |
| 99.99%       | ~52.6 minutes     | Four nines  |
| 99.999%      | ~5.26 minutes     | Five nines  |

Cloud providers achieve high availability through **redundancy** -
duplicate systems, data centers, and network paths so that no single
failure brings the service down. When a VM host fails in a properly
configured cloud environment, workloads automatically migrate to a
healthy host.

### 🤝 Shared Responsibility Model

The shared responsibility model defines **who is responsible for
securing what** in a cloud environment. The exact boundary shifts
depending on the service model - and the exam tests this directly.

| Responsibility                    | On-Premises | IaaS     | PaaS     | SaaS     |
|-----------------------------------|-------------|----------|----------|----------|
| Physical datacenter & hardware    | Customer    | Provider | Provider | Provider |
| Network infrastructure            | Customer    | Provider | Provider | Provider |
| Hypervisor / virtualization layer | Customer    | Provider | Provider | Provider |
| Operating system                  | Customer    | Customer | Provider | Provider |
| Runtime & middleware              | Customer    | Customer | Provider | Provider |
| Application                       | Customer    | Customer | Customer | Provider |
| Data & access management          | Customer    | Customer | Customer | Customer |

The key insight: **the customer always owns their data and is always
responsible for identity and access management**, regardless of the
service model. The provider takes on progressively more responsibility
as you move from IaaS → PaaS → SaaS, but they never take ownership of
the customer’s data.

> **💡 Pro Tip:** Know the difference between Type 1 vs Type 2
> hypervisors. - Be able to identify NAT, bridged, and host-only
> networking. - Remember: snapshots are NOT backups. - Elasticity =
> automatic scaling. High availability = minimal downtime through
> redundancy. Shared responsibility = customer always owns their data,
> even in SaaS.
>
> **⚠️ Exam Trap:** “The cloud provider is responsible for everything”
> is never correct. Even in a SaaS model, the customer owns their data,
> controls who has access to it, and is responsible for compliance with
> regulations like HIPAA and GDPR as they apply to that data.

**\**

## Quick Review— Virtualization & Cloud Computing

### Questions

1.  What is the key difference between a Type 1 and a Type 2 hypervisor,
    and give one example of each?

2.  A technician creates a snapshot of a VM before installing updates.
    The updates cause a critical failure. What can the technician do,
    and why are snapshots not considered backups?

3.  In the shared responsibility model, what does the customer always
    remain responsible for, regardless of whether the service is IaaS,
    PaaS, or SaaS?

4.  A company needs to automatically scale their web servers up during
    peak hours and down during off-hours without manual intervention.
    What cloud concept describes this capability?

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### Answer Key

1.  A Type 1 hypervisor runs directly on hardware without a host OS
    (most efficient, used in enterprise environments) — example: VMware
    ESXi, Microsoft Hyper-V Server. A Type 2 hypervisor runs on top of a
    host OS (easier to install, ideal for labs) — example: VirtualBox,
    VMware Workstation.

2.  The technician can revert the snapshot to instantly return the VM to
    its pre-update state. Snapshots are not backups because they live on
    the same physical disk as the VM — if the disk fails, both the VM
    and all snapshots are lost.

3.  The customer always remains responsible for their data and
    identity/access management (controlling who has access), regardless
    of the service model.

4.  Elasticity — the ability to automatically scale resources up or down
    in response to demand without manual intervention. This is distinct
    from scalability (which can be manual).

# 🖥️ Module: Desktop PC Hardware

## 🎯 Module Objectives

Desktop hardware is 25% of Core 1 – one of the largest domains. While
laptops get most hands-on attention, the exam tests desktop components,
form factors, power supply specs, and cooling in depth.

By the end of this module, learners will be able to:

- Identify and describe the major components inside a desktop PC.

- Differentiate between motherboard form factors (ATX, microATX,
  mini-ITX).

- Select the correct power supply wattage and connector types for a
  system.

- Understand CPU socket types, cooling solutions, and thermal
  management.

- Identify RAM types, slots, and installation rules (dual-channel).

- Recognize PCIe slot versions and their use cases.

- Understand case/chassis types and their trade-offs.

## 🎛 Motherboard Form Factors

The form factor determines the motherboard’s physical size, screw hole
positions, and how it fits into a case. Choosing compatible components
requires matching form factors.

| Form Factor | Dimensions | PCIe Slots | Best Use Case |
|----|----|----|----|
| ATX | 305 × 244 mm | 7 expansion slots | Full desktop towers — maximum expandability for workstations and gaming rigs. |
| Micro-ATX (mATX) | 244 × 244 mm | 4 expansion slots | Mid-size builds balancing size and expansion. Most common in pre-built systems. |
| Mini-ITX | 170 × 170 mm | 1 expansion slot | Compact/small form factor (SFF) builds. Limited cooling and expansion. |
| Extended ATX (E-ATX) | 305 × 330 mm | 8+ expansion slots | High-end workstations and servers needing maximum PCIe lanes. |

**Multisocket Motherboards** — Server/workstation boards with two or
more CPU sockets, allowing multiple physical processors to work together
for higher performance in demanding workloads. Common in servers, rare
in consumer desktops.

<img src="media/image17.png" style="width:5.52083in;height:9.0068in"
alt="Motherboard form factor comparison chart listing dimensions, PCIe slots, and use cases for E-ATX, ATX, mATX, and Mini-ITX." />

> **⚠️ Exam Trap:** CompTIA Core 1 loves to test you on identifying
> these physical components. Pay close attention to the difference
> between the main 24-pin power (#5) and the 8-pin CPU power (#4), as
> well as identifying PCIe slots vs. RAM slots!

### 🧮 Motherboard Components

Figure: ATX motherboard (ASUS TUF Gaming Z590-PLUS WIFI). Numbers 1–22
identify board components; letters A–K identify rear panel connectors.
See accompanying table for full descriptions and exam notes.

<td>17</td>
<td>Thunderbolt Header</td>

</tr>
<tr>
<td>18</td>
<td>Q-LEDs</td>
<td>Diagnostic LEDs that light up during POST failure. Indicate which
component is failing: CPU / DRAM / VGA / BOOT. Critical for no-POST

</tr>
<tr>
<td>19</td>
<td>PCH Heatsink</td>
<td>Covers the Platform Controller Hub - modern replacement for

</tr>
<tr>
<td>20</td>
<td>CMOS Battery</td>
<td>CR2032 coin cell. Powers BIOS clock and settings when PC is off.
Dead battery = clock resets every boot, BIOS settings lost. Replace
every 5-10 years.</td>
<td>High</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>21</td>
<td>Fan &amp; Pump Headers</td>
<td>CPU_FAN = required (system won’t boot without fan detected).
AIO_PUMP = liquid cooler pump. CHA_FAN 1/2/3 = optional case fans. 4-pin
= PWM speed control.</td>
<td>High</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>22</td>
<td>Rear Panel I/O</td>
<td>All external ports are on the back of the board. Includes USB,
video, audio, network, and PS/2. See rear panel section below.</td>
<td>Medium</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="4">Rear Panel Connectors (A–K)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>A</td>
<td>USB 2.0 Type-A</td>
<td>480 Mbps. Usually black colored. Still used for keyboards, mice, and
low-speed peripherals. Backward compatible with USB 1.1.</td>
<td>High</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>B</td>
<td>HDMI Port</td>
<td>Carries video and audio over single cable. 19-pin. Only available
when using CPU with integrated graphics (no discrete GPU required).</td>
<td>High</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>C</td>
<td>USB 3.2 Gen 2x2 Type-C</td>
<td>Fastest USB on this board - 20 Gbps. USB-C connector. Gen 2x2 = two
10 Gbps channels bonded. Note: not Thunderbolt.</td>
<td>Medium</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>D</td>
<td>USB 3.2 Gen 2 Type-A</td>
<td>10 Gbps. Usually red on ASUS boards to distinguish from Gen 1
(blue). Type-A = standard rectangular USB connector.</td>
<td>Medium</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>E</td>
<td>Wi-Fi Module (Wi-Fi 6)</td>
<td>802.11ax - up to 9.6 Gbps. Built-in Bluetooth. Antennas connect
here. Uses M.2 E-key slot internally. Managed via Device Manager in
Windows.</td>
<td>High</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>F</td>
<td>Optical S/PDIF OUT</td>
<td>Digital audio output using fiber optic cable. Immune to electrical
interference. Used for home theater receivers. Legacy but may appear on
exam.</td>
<td>Low</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>G</td>
<td>PS/2 Combo Port</td>
<td>Legacy keyboard/mouse port. NOT hot-swappable - must plug in before
boot. Purple = keyboard, green = mouse (older individual ports).</td>
<td>Medium</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>H</td>
<td>DisplayPort</td>
<td>20-pin latching connector. Replacement for VGA/DVI. Supports higher
refresh rates than HDMI. Can transmit via USB-C alt mode.</td>
<td>High</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>I</td>
<td>USB 3.2 Gen 1 Type-A</td>
<td>5 Gbps. Blue colored. Most common USB 3.x ports. SuperSpeed USB.
Backward compatible with USB 2.0 devices at slower speed.</td>
<td>High</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>J</td>
<td>Ethernet RJ-45 (2.5G)</td>
<td>2.5 Gbps wired network. RJ-45 connector. APIPA address (169.254.x.x)
= DHCP failure, not a physical port issue.</td>
<td>High</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>K</td>
<td>Audio Jacks (3.5mm)</td>
<td>Color coded: green = line out/speakers, pink = mic in, blue = line
in. Rear outputs for surround sound. Managed by onboard audio
codec.</td>
<td>Medium</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

*Priority guide: High = frequently tested on A+ exam. Medium = may
appear on exam. Low = good to know, rarely tested.*

## 💾 RAM (Random Access Memory)

### 📋 RAM Types

| DDR Type | Speed Range | Voltage | Key Notes |
|----|----|----|----|
| DDR4 | 2133–3200+ MHz | 1.2V | Current mainstream standard. Most desktops and laptops shipping 2016–2023. |
| DDR5 | 4800–7200+ MHz | 1.1V | Current high-performance standard. Required for Intel 12th gen+ and AMD Ryzen 7000+. Higher bandwidth and capacity. |
| DDR3 | 1066–1866 MHz | 1.35–1.5V | Legacy. Found in older systems. NOT compatible with DDR4/DDR5 slots. |
| ECC RAM | Varies | Varies | Error-Correcting Code RAM. Detects and corrects single-bit memory errors. Used in servers and workstations. |

<img src="media/image19.png" style="width:6.5in;height:5.08403in"
alt="DDR RAM comparison chart for DDR3, DDR4, and DDR5, highlighting notch positions, voltage, and speed differences for the CompTIA A+ exam." />

### 🔧 RAM Installation Rules

- Dual-Channel: Installing matched pairs of RAM in the correct slots
  (usually slots 1&3 or 2&4 - check motherboard manual) doubles memory
  bandwidth. Color-coded slots on most boards.

- Capacity: Install RAM in matched pairs (e.g., 2×8GB, 2×16GB) for
  dual-channel operation. Mismatched sizes default to single-channel.

- Compatibility: DDR4 and DDR5 slots are physically different and not
  interchangeable. DDR5 DIMMs have a different notch position.

- Max Capacity: Determined by the motherboard chipset and CPU, not just
  the number of slots.

## ⚡ Power Supply Unit (PSU)

### 📊 PSU Form Factors & Ratings

| Specification | Details & Exam Relevance |
|----|----|
| ATX PSU | Standard desktop PSU. Uses a 24-pin ATX connector to the motherboard and an 8-pin (or 4+4 pin) EPS connector for the CPU. |
| SFX PSU | Small Form Factor PSU. Used in compact cases. Physically smaller than ATX but uses the same connectors. |
| Wattage | Must be sufficient for all components. Rule of thumb: Add up TDP of CPU + GPU + storage + fans, then add 20-30% headroom. |
| 80 PLUS Rating | The 80 PLUS program certifies PSUs at 20%, 50%, and 100% load (Titanium also adds 10%). Efficiency means how much wall power is converted into usable DC power for components. Higher = less heat and lower electricity cost. |
| Modular vs. Non-Modular | Modular PSUs allow unused cables to be removed, improving airflow and cable management. Semi-modular keeps essential cables attached. |

> **⚠️ Exam Trap:** A PSU with insufficient wattage causes random
> shutdowns and instability - symptoms that look like software or RAM
> errors. Always check PSU wattage when troubleshooting unexplained
> crashes.
>
> **💡 Pro Tip:** Why 80 Plus Matters. Higher ratings = less wasted
> energy as heat, quieter cooling, and lower electricity draw. Savings
> are modest (about \$5–8/year between Bronze and Gold for a typical
> gaming PC), but higher rated PSUs often use better components and last
> longer. Gold is the sweet spot for most builds — balancing cost,
> efficiency, and reliability.

<img src="media/image20.png" style="width:6.5in;height:5.54236in"
alt="A detailed CompTIA A+ study infographic for understanding a computer Power Supply Unit (PSU). The diagram features a 750-watt specification label emphasizing the +12V rail, an 80 PLUS efficiency hierarchy chart showing peak efficiencies at 50% load, troubleshooting tips for failing PSUs, and labeled visual examples of modular cables, including 24-pin ATX, SATA, PCIe, CPU, and Molex connectors" />

### 🔌 PSU Connector Types

| Connector | Purpose |
|----|----|
| 8-pin EPS (4+4 pin) | CPU power. Provides dedicated 12V power to the processor VRM. Some high-end boards use dual 8-pin. |
| 24-pin ATX | Main motherboard power connector. Provides 3.3V, 5V, and 12V rails to the board. |
| 6+2 pin PCIe | Dedicated GPU power. Mid-range GPUs use one connector; high-end GPUs may require two or three. |
| SATA Power | 15-pin connector for SATA HDDs, SSDs, and optical drives. |
| Molex (4-pin) | Legacy connector for older HDDs, fans, and case lighting. Mostly obsolete but may appear on older systems. |

<img src="media/image21.png" style="width:6.5in;height:3.78056in"
alt="Diagram illustrating five PSU connector types used in computer hardware, each with color-coded sections and labeled pin configurations. Connectors include 8-pin EPS (4+4) for CPU power, 24-pin ATX for motherboard power, SATA power for storage drives, 6+2 pin PCIe for graphics cards, and Molex (4-pin) for legacy power, highlighting pin counts, shapes, and typical usage scenarios." />

## 🎮 Expansion Cards & PCIe

### 📐 PCIe Slot Versions & Speeds

| PCIe Version | Speed per Lane (x1) | Speed at x16 | Common Use |
|----|----|----|----|
| PCIe 3.0 | ~1 GB/s | ~16 GB/s | GPUs, NVMe SSDs (still common in many systems). |
| PCIe 4.0 | ~2 GB/s | ~32 GB/s | High-performance NVMe SSDs, modern GPUs. |
| PCIe 5.0 | ~4 GB/s | ~64 GB/s | Cutting-edge NVMe SSDs, next-gen GPUs. |
| PCIe 6.0 | ~8 GB/s | ~128 GB/s | Emerging standard — data center and future consumer use. |

### 

### 🃏 Common Expansion Card Types

| Card Type | Purpose & Notes |
|----|----|
| GPU (Video Card) | Handles display output and graphics rendering. Connects to PCIe x16 slot. Requires separate PCIe power from PSU. |
| Sound Card | Dedicated audio processing. Used when onboard audio is insufficient for professional audio work. |
| NIC (Network Card) | Adds wired or wireless networking. Used when onboard NIC is insufficient or fails. |
| RAID Controller | Manages RAID arrays in hardware, and offloading processing from the CPU. |
| Capture Card | Records or streams video input from external sources (cameras, gaming consoles). |
| USB Expansion Card | Adds additional USB ports (especially USB 3.x or USB4) when onboard ports are insufficient. |
| Thunderbolt Add-In Card | Adds Thunderbolt connectivity to systems without native support. |

## 🌡️ System Cooling & Airflow

Proper airflow is critical for system stability. Poor cooling leads to
thermal throttling, random shutdowns, and reduced component lifespan.

| Cooling Concept | Explanation |
|----|----|
| Positive vs. Negative Pressure | Positive pressure: more intake than exhaust fans - keeps dust out. Negative pressure: more exhaust than intake - better airflow but draws in unfiltered air. |
| Front-to-Back Airflow | Standard airflow pattern: cool air enters from front/bottom, hot air exits from rear/top. Components should be oriented to match this flow. |
| Case Fans | Typically 120mm or 140mm. More fans = better cooling but more noise. Fan headers on motherboard: CPU_FAN (required), SYS_FAN (optional). |
| CPU TDP (Thermal Design Power) | Maximum heat output in watts the cooling system must handle. A 65W TDP CPU needs a cooler rated at least 65W. |
| Dust Filters | Prevent dust accumulation on components. Clean every 3–6 months using compressed air. |

## 📦 Case / Chassis Form Factors

| Case Type | Compatible Motherboards & Notes |
|----|----|
| Full Tower | Supports E-ATX, ATX, mATX, Mini-ITX. Maximum space for components, cooling, and cable management. |
| Mid Tower | Supports ATX, mATX, Mini-ITX. Most common case type. Good balance of size and expandability. |
| Mini Tower / SFF | Supports mATX or Mini-ITX. Compact footprint. Limited cooling and expansion options. |
| Rack Mount (1U/2U/4U) | Used in server environments. Measured in rack units (U). Slides into standard 19-inch server racks. |

<img src="media/image22.png" style="width:6.5in;height:4.21765in"
alt="A detailed CompTIA A+ study infographic for understanding a computer Power Supply Unit (PSU). The diagram features a 750-watt specification label emphasizing the +12V rail, an 80 PLUS efficiency hierarchy chart showing peak efficiencies at 50% load, troubleshooting tips for failing PSUs, and labeled visual examples of modular cables, including 24-pin ATX, SATA, PCIe, CPU, and Molex connectors" />

**ℹ️ Exam Tip:** Memorize PCIe slot sizes: x1 (short), x4 (medium), x8
(medium), x16 (long). GPUs always use x16 slots. - ATX power connector
is 24-pin to motherboard + 8-pin to CPU - know both connectors. - DDR4
and DDR5 are NOT interchangeable - different notch positions prevent
accidental insertion. - Dual-channel requires matched DIMMs in the
correct slot pair - check the motherboard manual. - A dead CMOS battery
= system clock resets on every boot and BIOS settings are lost. - PSU
failure symptoms: random shutdowns, won’t power on, burning smell -
always replace, never repair

## 🧠 CPU (Central Processing Unit)

### 🔌 CPU Socket Types

The CPU socket on the motherboard must match the CPU’s socket type.
Sockets are generation-specific - always verify compatibility before
purchasing.

| Socket | Manufacturer | Notes |
|----|----|----|
| LGA 1700 | Intel | Used by Intel 12th, 13th, and 14th gen Core processors. LGA (Land Grid Array) - pins on motherboard. |
| LGA 1851 | Intel | Launched 2024 for Arrow Lake (“Core Ultra Series 2”) desktop CPUs; DDR5-only. |
| AM4 | AMD | Used by AMD Ryzen 1000-5000 series. One of the longest-lasting sockets in history using PGA (Pin Grid Array) -pins on CPU socket. |
| AM5 | AMD | Used by AMD Ryzen 7000 series and newer. LGA-style (AMD moved pins to motherboard). AM5 is expected to support multiple future Ryzen generations |
| SP3 / SP5 | AMD (Server) | SP3 is used for EPYC Naples/Rome/Milan and SP5 is used for EPYC Genoa/Bergamo (Zen 4 server). Distinct sockets for different EPYC generations, not interchangeable. |
| LGA 4677 | Intel (Server) | Used in Intel Xeon Scalable server processors. |

alt="CPU socket type comparison chart detailing PGA vs LGA architectures, including risk factors, handling best practices, and a PGA/LGA match-up summary." />

### ❄️ CPU Cooling Solutions

| Cooling Type | Description & Notes |
|----|----|
| Stock / Boxed Cooler | Included with most non-K Intel and AMD CPUs. Adequate for stock speeds. Not recommended for overclocking. Intel “K” series and high‑end AMD often ship without a cooler. |
| Aftermarket Air Cooler | Third-party heatsink + fan. Better thermal performance than stock. Varies widely in size and TDP rating. Large tower coolers may require case clearance checks. |
| All-in-One (AIO) Liquid Cooler | Closed-loop liquid cooling with a pump, radiator, and fans. Excellent performance. Requires radiator mounting space in case. Lifespan limited by pump reliability (typically 5–7 years). |
| Custom Water-Cooling Loop | Fully custom liquid cooling. Maximum performance. Complex installation. Used in enthusiast/overclocking builds. |
| Passive Cooling | No fans - relies on heatsink mass and airflow. Used in low-power/silent systems. Not suitable for high-TDP CPUs. |

## 🎮 Graphics Cards & GPUs

### 🖥️ Integrated vs. Discrete Graphics

| Type | Description | Key Detail |
|----|----|----|
| Integrated (Onboard/APU) | GPU built into the CPU or motherboard chipset. | Shares system RAM; lower power draw; sufficient for basic tasks (web browsing, office work). |
| Discrete (Dedicated) | Separate expansion card with its own GPU and VRAM. | Required for gaming, video editing, 3D rendering, and AI/ML workloads; installs via PCIe slot. |

> **💡 Pro Tip:** APU” (Accelerated Processing Unit) is AMD’s term for a
> CPU with integrated graphics built in. Intel’s equivalent is simply
> called integrated graphics (e.g., Intel Iris Xe, Intel Arc
> integrated).

### 🧠 Video RAM (VRAM)

VRAM is dedicated memory on the graphics card used to store textures,
frame buffers, and rendering data. More VRAM allows for higher
resolutions, more detailed textures, and better multitasking across
multiple displays.

Unlike integrated graphics — which borrow from system RAM and compete
with the CPU for memory bandwidth — a discrete card’s VRAM is separate,
dedicated, and doesn’t slow down other system processes.

### 🔌 Expansion Slot & Installation

| Component | Notes |
|----|----|
| PCIe x16 slot | Standard interface for discrete graphics cards; PCIe generation (3.0/4.0/5.0/6.0) affects available bandwidth. |
| Auxiliary power | Higher-end cards require additional power via 6-pin or 8-pin PCIe power connectors directly from the PSU. |
| Physical clearance | Larger cards require adequate case space and airflow clearance. |

> **ℹ️ Exam Tip:** A graphics card drawing more power than the
> motherboard’s PCIe slot alone can supply (75W) will require a
> supplemental power connector — failing to connect it is a common cause
> of a card not powering on or the system failing to boot.

### 🎥 Output Ports

Graphics cards use the same standard video connectors covered in the
Display Technologies module — HDMI, DisplayPort, DVI, and VGA — but here
the GPU acts as the *source* device. Multiple output ports on a single
card allow for multi-monitor setups.

### 🌬️ Cooling

| Method | Description |
|----|----|
| Air (Blower) | Single fan channels hot air out the back of the case; simpler, often louder. |
| Air (Open-air) | Multiple fans vent heat into the case interior; better cooling, requires good case airflow. |
| Liquid (AIO) | Closed-loop liquid cooling for high-end cards; quieter and more efficient at managing heat. |

> **💡 Pro Tip:** Thermal throttling occurs when a GPU exceeds safe
> operating temperatures. The card automatically reduces clock speed to
> cool down, resulting in a performance drop.

### 💾 Drivers

GPU drivers must be installed and updated separately from the operating
system — NVIDIA, AMD, and Intel each provide their own driver packages.
Outdated or corrupted drivers are among the most common causes of
display artifacts, crashes, or a card not being detected properly by the
OS.

🛠️ Common Troubleshooting Symptoms

| Symptom | Likely Cause / Fix |
|----|----|
| No display output | Check card seating in the slot, auxiliary power connections, and monitor cable/input source. |
| Artifacts or glitches on screen | Often a driver issue, overheating, or a failing card. |
| System won’t boot with discrete card installed | BIOS/UEFI may still be set to use integrated graphics as the primary display — check boot display settings. |
| Card not detected by OS | Reseat the card, check power connectors, or reinstall/update drivers. |

> **ℹ️ Exam Tip:** If a system boots fine with onboard graphics but
> shows no display once a discrete card is installed, check the
> BIOS/UEFI setting for primary display adapter — it may still be
> pointed at the integrated GPU instead of the PCIe slot.

## 🖥️ Custom PC Configurations

| Build Type | Priority Components | Key Consideration |
|----|----|----|
| Graphic/CAD Workstation | High-end multi-core CPU, large RAM, professional GPU (ECC memory support) | Precision over speed; often needs ECC RAM |
| Audio/Video Editing | Fast multi-core CPU, large/fast storage (NVMe), color-accurate display | High storage throughput and capacity |
| Virtualization Host | Max CPU cores/threads, maximum RAM, fast storage | Needs virtualization extensions (VT-x/AMD-V) enabled in BIOS |
| Gaming PC | High-end GPU, fast single-core CPU performance, high refresh-rate display | GPU is the priority component |
| Home Theater PC (HTPC) | Small form factor case, quiet/passive cooling, HDMI output | Low noise output is critical |
| Thin Client | Minimal CPU/RAM, no local storage needed | Relies on network/server for processing |
| NAS (Network Attached Storage) | RAID-capable storage, reliable NIC, low-power CPU | Redundancy and always-on reliability matter most |

## Quick Review — PC Hardware Components

### Questions

1.  What is the difference between the 24-pin ATX connector and the
    8-pin EPS connector on a motherboard?

2.  A desktop PC’s system clock resets every time the computer is
    powered off. What component has failed, and what is the replacement?

3.  What is the key difference between DDR4 and DDR5 RAM in terms of
    physical compatibility?

4.  A user installs 4x8GB DDR4 RAM sticks but the system only shows 16GB
    available. What is the most likely cause, and how should the
    technician verify?

5.  A gaming PC randomly shuts down during intensive gaming sessions.
    Temps are normal, RAM tests pass. What is the most likely cause?

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### Answer Key

1.  The 24-pin ATX connector provides main power to the motherboard
    (3.3V, 5V, and 12V rails) — the system will not POST without it. The
    8-pin EPS connector provides dedicated 12V power specifically to the
    CPU. Both must be connected for the system to function.

2.  The CMOS battery (CR2032 coin cell) has failed. It powers the BIOS
    clock and settings when the PC is off. Replace with a new CR2032
    battery.

3.  DDR4 and DDR5 have different notch positions and are not physically
    interchangeable — you cannot install DDR4 RAM in a DDR5 slot or vice
    versa. The motherboard supports only one type.

4.  Most likely: RAM is not installed in the correct slots for
    dual-channel operation, or one channel has failed. Verify by
    checking BIOS/UEFI to see if all DIMMs are detected. Test RAM sticks
    individually to isolate a bad module.

5.  Insufficient PSU wattage. The power supply cannot deliver enough
    power under full GPU/CPU load, causing protective shutdowns.
    Calculate total system power draw and upgrade PSU if needed.

# 🖥️ Module: Computer Displays

## 🎯 Module Objectives

By the end of this module, learners will be able to:

- Differentiate between major display panel technologies: LCD, LED,
  OLED, and their variants.

- Identify the function of an inverter and a digitizer and their role in
  display assembly.

- Explain key display attributes such as resolution, refresh rate, and
  pixel density.

- Identify common projector connection types and troubleshoot typical
  projector issues (overheating, bulb failure, image/input problems).

- Compare display configuration modes for multi-monitor and projector
  setups (duplicate, extend, external only).

## 🖥️ Display Technologies

| Technology | Description | Variants / Features |
|----|----|----|
| LCD (Liquid Crystal Display) | Uses liquid crystals between polarized glass panes. Requires backlighting. | TN: Fastest, cheapest, worst color. IPS: Best color, widest viewing angle. VA: Best contrast ratio. |
| LED | Technically an LCD that uses LEDs for backlighting instead of older fluorescent lamps (CCFL). | More power efficient. LEDs are DC devices, requiring driver circuits for stable operation. |
| OLED (Organic LED) | Uses organic compounds that emit their own light. Self-emissive pixels. | No backlighting needed. Truly black colors. Common in smartphones and foldables. |
| QLED (Quantum Dot LED) | LCD display that uses a quantum-dot enhanced LED backlight. | Higher brightness, vibrant and more saturated colors. It’s still LCD — not self‑emissive like OLED |
| Mini-LED | Advanced LED backlight technology using thousands of tiny LEDs. | Precise local dimming zones. Higher contrast than traditional LED. |
| CCFL (Cold Cathode Fluorescent Lamp) | Older backlight technology used before LED. | Requires inverter to convert DC to AC. Bulkier and hotter. Obsolete in modern systems. |

> **💡 Pro Tip:** LCD itself is the umbrella term — TN/IPS/VA are panel
> types. OLED is susceptible to burn‑in with static images. Mini-LED is
> often marketed as ‘near‑OLED’ contrast, but still LCD‑based.

## 👆 Inverter and Digitizer

- Inverter: A circuit board that converts Direct Current (DC) from the
  laptop to Alternating Current (AC). If an LCD display goes dark but an
  external monitor works, the inverter may be faulty. **LED-backlit and
  OLED displays do NOT require inverters, only CCFL‑backlit LCDs.**

- Digitizer: The component that registers touches and touch gestures
  (swiping, pinching) on a touchscreen. Works by monitoring the
  capacitance of a conductive layer. If integrated, you must often
  replace the entire panel.

## 🎥Projectors

Projectors extend the same video output as monitors but introduce unique
connection and maintenance considerations specific to display hardware.

### Connection Types

Projectors use the same standard video connectors as monitors:

| Connector   | Notes                                                       |
|-------------|-------------------------------------------------------------|
| HDMI        | Most common on modern projectors; carries audio + video     |
| VGA         | Still common on older/budget projectors; analog, video only |
| DVI         | Less common on projectors; digital video only               |
| DisplayPort | Found on higher-end business/professional projectors        |

### \

### Common Troubleshooting Symptoms

| Symptom | Likely Cause / Fix |
|----|----|
| Intermittent shutdown | Overheating. Check for a clogged/dirty air filter restricting airflow; projector shuts down automatically to prevent damage |
| Burnt-out bulb (lamp) | Lamps have a limited lifespan and dim/fail over time — replace the projector bulb |
| Fuzzy or blurry image | Lens isn’t properly focused-adjust the focus ring; also check resolution/scaling settings |
| Distorted image | Incorrect aspect ratio or resolution setting; keystone correction may be needed if projector is angled |
| Dead pixels | Physical defect in the imaging chip/panel -generally not user repairable |
| Flashing screen | Loose cable connection or failing video source/adapter |
| Sizing issues | Wrong aspect ratio selected, or keystoning from an off-angle placement |
| Incorrect input source / “No Signal” | Wrong input selected on the projector — cycle through input sources to match the connected cable |
| Audio issues | Check cable connection, mute/volume settings, and whether the projector or an external speaker system is handling audio output |

### Maintenance Basics

- **Bulb/lamp replacement** — lamps degrade with use and eventually burn
  out; always allow the projector to cool before replacing

- **Air filter cleaning** — a dirty filter is one of the most common
  causes of overheating and unexpected shutdown

- **Ventilation** — ensure adequate clearance around intake/exhaust
  vents to prevent heat buildup

> **ℹ️ Exam Tip:** Projector-specific troubleshooting (bulb failure,
> overheating shutdown, filter maintenance) is a common way exam
> questions distinguish projector issues from standard monitor issues —
> monitors don’t have bulbs or air filters to worry about.

## 📐 Display Attributes

| Attribute | Definition | Key Detail |
|----|----|----|
| Resolution | Number of pixels horizontally × vertically (e.g., 1920×1080). | Native Resolution is the one at which the display looks its best. |
| Pixel Density | Number of pixels per linear unit (e.g., per inch). | Higher density = greater perceived detail. |
| Refresh Rate | How many times the display redraws per second, measured in Hertz (Hz). | Faster refresh rates (120 Hz+) provide a more fluid experience. |
| Color Depth | Number of bits per pixel for color information. | 8-bit color gives approximately 16.7 million possible colors. |
| Color Gamut | Range of visible colors a monitor can display. | Often expressed as a percentage of sRGB or Adobe RGB. |

## 🖧 Multiple Display Setup

| Mode | Description |
|----|----|
| Internal Display Only | Uses only the laptop screen. |
| Duplicate / Clone | The external display shows the same content as the internal one (useful for presentations). |
| Extend | The external display expands the virtual desktop across both monitors. |
| External Only | Disables the laptop screen and uses only the external monitor. |

# 💾 Module: Hard Drives & Storage Technologies

## 🎯 Module Objectives

By the end of this module, learners will be able to:

- Identify and differentiate between primary storage device types,
  including HDDs, SSDs, and hybrid technologies.

- Understand common storage interfaces and connections, including SATA,
  NVMe, and PCIe.

- Differentiate between MBR (Master Boot Record) and GPT (GUID Partition
  Table) partition formats.

- Describe RAID concepts, including striping, mirroring, and parity.

## 📊 Storage Drive Comparison

| Feature | HDD | SSD (SATA) | SSHD - Hybrid | M.2 NVMe SSD |
|----|----|----|----|----|
| Technology | Mechanical (spinning platters, moving read/write heads) | Electronic (NAND flash memory chips) | Combines a traditional spinning HDD with a small amount of NAND flash cache | Electronic (NAND flash memory chips) connected directly via PCIe bus; bypasses the SATA bottleneck |
| Cost | More affordable per GB | More expensive per GB | Mid-range — more than HDD, less than SSD | Most expensive per GB of consumer drives |
| Performance | Slower read/write speeds, higher latency | Much faster, very low latency | Faster than HDD for frequently accessed data (cached on flash); slower than SSD overall | Fastest consumer storage — 3,500-7,000+ MB/s read/write (vs. 600 MB/s max for SATA SSD) |
| Durability | Lower durability; susceptible to physical shock | Higher durability; no moving parts | Same as HDD — still has moving parts susceptible to physical shock | High durability; no moving parts. Compact M.2 form factor (no cables needed) |
| Power/Heat | Consumes more power and generates more heat | Consumes less power and generates less heat | Similar to HDD — spinning platters still consume power and generate heat | Low power consumption but can run hotter than SATA SSDs under sustained load. Many motherboards include M.2 heatsinks |

## 🔌 Storage Interfaces & Form Factors

| Connection | Speed Range | Form Factor | Best Use Cases |
|----|----|----|----|
| SATA III | Up to 6 Gb/s | 2.5”, 3.5” | General storage, OS drives on older systems. |
| M.2 SATA | Up to 6 Gb/s | M.2 slot | Ultrabooks, slim PCs needing SATA performance. |
| M.2 NVMe (PCIe) | 3,500–7,000+ MB/s | M.2 slot | High-performance systems, gaming, virtualization. |
| PCIe Add-in Card | Highest (PCIe x4/x8) | Full PCIe card | Workstations, servers, high-desktops. |
| SAS | 6–12 Gb/s | 2.5”, 3.5” | Servers, enterprise storage arrays. |
| USB External (3.0/C) | 5–20 Gb/s | External enclosure | Portable drives, backups. |
| Thunderbolt 3/4 | Up to 40 Gbps | USB-C enclosure | Extremely fast external storage. |

> **💡 Pro Tip:** SATA III = 6 Gb/s theoretical, ~600 MB/s practical
> throughput. NVMe speeds vary by PCIe generation (Gen 3 ≈ 3,500 MB/s,
> Gen 4 ≈ 7,000 MB/s, Gen 5 ≈ 12,000 MB/s emerging).

<img src="media/image26.png" style="width:6.5in;height:5.16736in"
alt="Storage Drive Comparison: HDD vs SSD vs M.2 NVMe&quot; keeps the nomenclature identical to your other successful, high-yield overview graphics (like your Printer Types or Motherboard Form Factors charts)." />

## 🗂️ Partition Table Formats: MBR vs. GPT

Before an operating system can use a storage drive, the drive must be
**partitioned** - divided into logical sections the OS can read, write,
and boot from. The partition table is a data structure written at the
very beginning of the drive that describes how those sections are
organized. There are two standards the A+ exam tests: the legacy **MBR**
and the modern **GPT**.

### 📋 MBR vs. GPT Comparison

| Feature | MBR (Master Boot Record) | GPT (GUID Partition Table) |
|----|----|----|
| Full Name | Master Boot Record | GUID Partition Table |
| Age / Origin | Legacy - introduced with IBM PC DOS in 1983 | Modern - part of the UEFI standard (2000s) |
| Max Drive Size | 2 TB - drives larger than 2 TB cannot be fully used | 9.4 ZB (effectively unlimited for current hardware) |
| Max Partitions | 4 primary partitions (or 3 primary + 1 extended with logical drives) | 128 primary partitions (no extended/logical distinction) |
| Boot Firmware | Requires BIOS (Legacy Boot) | Requires UEFI - will not boot on BIOS-only systems |
| Redundancy | Single partition table - if it’s corrupted, the drive is unreadable | Stores a backup copy of the partition table at the end of the drive |
| OS Support | Windows XP and earlier; Linux; older macOS | Windows 8/10/11 (64-bit); modern Linux; macOS (all Intel/Apple Silicon Macs) |
| Drive ID Method | Partition identified by physical location on disk | Each partition assigned a globally unique identifier (GUID) |

### 🔍 The Key Details

**MBR’s 4-partition limit** was worked around for decades using
*extended partitions* - a container partition holding multiple *logical
drives*. This is legacy knowledge but still appears on the exam.

**GPT’s 128-partition limit** is functionally unlimited for real-world
use. Windows only displays 128, but the spec allows more.

**UEFI vs. BIOS** is the deciding factor when choosing a partition
style. A modern Windows 11 install *requires* UEFI + GPT. If you see a
scenario where Windows 11 won’t install, check whether the drive is MBR
and whether Secure Boot / UEFI is enabled in the firmware.

A drive formatted as MBR cannot boot Windows in UEFI mode, and a drive
formatted as GPT cannot boot in Legacy BIOS mode. If a system boots to a
“bootmgr is missing” or “No bootable device” error after a drive swap,
the first thing to check is whether the partition style matches the
firmware mode set in BIOS/UEFI.

> **💡 Pro Tip:** In Windows, you can check a drive’s partition style at
> any time: **Disk Management** → right-click the disk number (not the
> volume) → **Properties** → **Volumes** tab → Partition style shows MBR
> or GPT. Alternatively, diskpart → list disk - a disk with an asterisk
> (\*) in the GPT column is GPT-formatted.
>
> **⚠️ Exam Trap:** MBR’s 2 TB limit is absolute - a 4 TB drive with an
> MBR partition table will only show ~2 TB of usable space. The
> remaining capacity is inaccessible without converting to GPT.

### 🔄 Converting Between MBR and GPT

| Scenario | Tool & Method |
|----|----|
| During Windows installation | Windows Setup → “Drive Options (Advanced)” → Delete all partitions → select drive → Convert appears automatically. Conversion option only appears if UEFI mode is enabled in firmware. |
| From Windows (non-destructive, Windows 10 1703+) | mbr2gpt /convert command - converts without data loss if prerequisites are met |
| From command line (destructive) | diskpart → clean → convert gpt - erases all data |
| From macOS | Disk Utility → Erase → select GUID Partition Map as the scheme |

## 🧱 RAID Configurations

<table>
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 14%" />
<col style="width: 27%" />
<col style="width: 27%" />
<col style="width: 0%" />
<col style="width: 29%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th>RAID Level</th>
<th>Technique</th>
<th>Fault Tolerance</th>
<th colspan="2">Best Use Cases</th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td>RAID 0</td>
<td>Striping only</td>
<td colspan="2">None — one drive failure loses all data.</td>
<td>Video editing, scratch disks, temporary workloads.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>RAID 1</td>
<td>Mirroring</td>
<td>Survives one drive failure.</td>
<td colspan="2">Small servers, OS volumes needing high reliability.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>RAID 5</td>
<td>Striping + single parity</td>
<td>Survives one drive failure (min 3 drives).</td>
<td colspan="2">General NAS, balanced performance &amp; redundancy.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>RAID 6</td>
<td>Striping + double parity</td>
<td>Survives two drive failures (min 4 drives).</td>
<td colspan="2">Large arrays prioritizing reliability.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>RAID 10</td>
<td>Mirroring + striping</td>
<td>Excellent (Survives one failure per mirrored pair).</td>
<td colspan="2">Databases, virtualization hosts.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>JBOD</td>
<td colspan="4">Just a Bunch of Disks/Drives (JBOD) is a storage
architecture that treats each disk as an independent unit rather than a
redundant array. When configured to function as a single logical drive,
it uses spanning: data is written sequentially to one drive until it
reaches capacity, then seamlessly flows to the next drive. Unlike RAID,
data is not striped, mirrored, or parity-protected. Good for
non-critical data, simple capacity expansion.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

> **⚠️ Exam Trap:** RAID is not a backup. A RAID array protects against
> drive failure but not against accidental deletion, ransomware, file
> corruption, or theft - all of which affect every drive in the array
> simultaneously. Always maintain a separate backup strategy alongside
> RAID.

# 💾 Module: Windows File Systems

## 🎯 Module Objectives

By the end of this module, learners will be able to:

- Identify and compare the three primary Windows file systems: NTFS,
  FAT32, and exFAT.
- Explain the capabilities and limitations of each file system.
- Convert drives between file systems using appropriate tools.

## 📊 File System Comparison

| Feature | NTFS | FAT32 | exFAT |
|----|----|----|----|
| Max File Size | 16 TB | 4 GB (hard limit) | 16 EB (unlimited) |
| Max Volume Size | 256 TB | 2 TB | 128 PB |
| File Permissions | Yes (ACLs) | No | No |
| Encryption (EFS) | Yes | No | No |
| Compression | Yes | No | No |
| Disk Quotas | Yes | No | No |
| Journaling | Yes | No | No |
| macOS Compatible | Read-only | Full | Full |
| Linux Compatible | Yes (with driver) | Full | Full (with driver) |
| Best For | Windows OS drives, internal storage | Legacy devices, UEFI boot partition | USB drives, SD cards, cross- platform |

> **💡 Pro Tip:** NTFS theoretical max file size is 16 EB, but Windows
> implementations cap it lower (~16 TB practical). FAT32 max volume size
> is technically 16 TB, but Windows limits formatting to 2 TB. exFAT is
> designed for removable storage, not OS drives.

## 🔍 Key File System Facts

**NTFS** is the required file system for Windows operating system
drives. It is the only file system that supports Windows security
features:

- File and folder permissions (ACLs) for granular access control
- Encrypting File System (EFS) for file-level encryption
- Disk quotas limiting per-user storage consumption
- Journaling for crash recovery (logs changes before committing)
- Volume Shadow Copy Service (VSS) for restore points and “Previous
  Versions”
- Support for files larger than 4 GB

**FAT32** is a legacy file system with a hard 4 GB maximum file size
limit. It has no security features (no permissions, no encryption, no
journaling). Its key advantage is universal compatibility across all
operating systems and devices. The UEFI System Partition (ESP) is always
FAT32.

**exFAT** is the modern replacement for FAT32 on removable media. It
removes the 4 GB file limit while maintaining cross-platform
compatibility (Windows, macOS, Linux). Like FAT32, it has no permissions
or journaling.

## 🧩 File System Troubleshooting

| Symptom | Likely Cause | Fix |
|----|----|----|
| “File is too large for the destination file system” | Drive is FAT32; file exceeds 4 GB | Reformat drive to exFAT or NTFS |
| Cannot set file permissions on external drive | Drive is FAT32 or exFAT | Reformat to NTFS if permissions needed |
| Drive shows as RAW in Disk Management | File system corrupted | Run chkdsk /f or reformat |
| “Access Denied” on drive moved from another PC | NTFS permissions tied to old user SID | Take ownership (Properties, Security, Advanced, Change Owner) |

## 🔄 Converting Between File Systems

| Conversion | Method | Data Loss? |
|----|----|----|
| FAT32 to NTFS | convert X: /fs:ntfs (command line) X=Drive Letter | No (non- destructive) |
| NTFS to FAT32 | Must reformat the drive | Yes (all data erased) |
| Any to exFAT | Must reformat the drive | Yes (all data erased) |

**ℹ️ Exam Tip:** “File too large” error on a USB drive with free space =
FAT32’s 4 GB limit. NTFS is the ONLY file system supporting Windows file
permissions. If a question mentions security, ACLs, or EFS, the answer
is NTFS. The UEFI System Partition (ESP) is always FAT32, even on GPT
drives. The convert command is one-way: FAT32 to NTFS works
non-destructively. NTFS to FAT32 requires reformatting. exFAT is the
correct answer when cross-platform AND large files are needed on
removable media.

# 🔌 Module: Cables, Connectors, & Adapters

## 🎯 Module Objectives

By the end of this module, learners will be able to:

- Identify and distinguish between copper and fiber-optic network cables
  and their associated connectors.

- Compare and contrast the different USB and Thunderbolt standards and
  types.

- Recognize legacy and modern video interfaces, including HDMI,
  DisplayPort, DVI, and VGA.

- Differentiate between hard drive cabling standards: SATA, SAS, and
  IDE.

## 🔗 Ethernet Cable Categories

| Category | Speed and Distance | Shielding & Jacket Types |
|----|----|----|
| Cat 5e | Supports 1 Gbps up to 100 m. | UTP (Unshielded Twisted Pair): No protection from interference. |
| Cat 6 | Supports 1 Gbps up to 100 m or 10 Gbps up to 55 m. | STP (Shielded Twisted Pair): Protective shield against EMI and RFI. |
| Cat 6a | Handles 10 Gbps at the full 100 m length. | CMR (Riser): Fire-resistant jacket for running cables between floors. |
| Cat 8 / 8.1 / 8.2 | Supports 25 to 40 Gbps over ~30 m. | \*CMP (Plenum): Jacket that produces less smoke in a fire, suited for HVAC ducts. |

**Note:** CMP is a jacket fire-rating applicable to any cable category
(Cat5e, Cat6, etc.). CMP information is not likely to be on exam but is
good to know for real‑world installers.

> **⚠️ Exam Trap:** Cat6 *can* do 10 Gbps, but only at a shorter 55m
> distance. Cat6a is the one that gets you full 10 Gbps at the full
> 100m.

<span id="_Toc235130339" class="anchor"></span>

## 🏵 Legacy Connectors

| Term | Description |
|----|----|
| T568A / T568B | Wiring standards defining pin-out order for RJ45 termination. Same standard both ends = straight-through cable. Different standard each end = crossover cable. |
| F-Type Connector | Threaded coaxial connector used for cable TV, cable internet (DOCSIS), and satellite. |
| DB9 | 9-pin D-subminiature connector, historically serial (RS-232). Found on legacy equipment and some console ports. |
| Network Tap | Hardware device inserted into a physical connection that passively copies traffic for monitoring, without disrupting data flow. |

## 💡 Fiber Optic Cables

| Type | Description | Connectors |
|----|----|----|
| Single-mode | A single ray of light travels down the core tube. | More expensive transceivers; used for longer distances. |
| Multimode | Multiple light beams travel down a larger core tube. | Less expensive transceivers; often used for shorter distances. |

Common connectors: ST (Straight Tip), LC (Lucent Connector), and SC
(Subscriber/Square Connector).

## ⚡ USB & Thunderbolt Standards

| Standard | Version (Speed) | Connector Type | Key Feature / Notes |
|----|----|----|----|
| USB 2.0 | 480 Mbps (2001) | Type A, B, Mini, Micro | Cables max out at 5 m. |
| USB 3.x | SuperSpeed (5/10 Gbps) | Type A, B, Micro, Type C | 3.x connectors are usually blue. |
| USB 4.0 | Up to 40 Gbps | Type C only | Supports alt mode (tunneling DisplayPort, HDMI, PCIe). |
| Thunderbolt 1/2 | Up to 20 Gbps | Mini DisplayPort | Compatible with DisplayPort. |
| Thunderbolt 3/4 | Up to 40 Gbps | USB Type C | Thunderbolt 4 supports two 4K displays; Thunderbolt 3 supports one. |
| Thunderbolt 5 | Up to 80 Gbps | USB Type C | Supports 240W charging and dual 8K monitors. |

### ⚡ USB Power Delivery 3.1

USB Power Delivery (USB PD) is a charging protocol that allows for
significantly higher power transfer over USB-C cables compared to
standard USB.

| Version | Max Power | Use Cases |
|----|----|----|
| USB PD 2.0 | Up to 100W (20V @ 5A) | Laptops, tablets, monitors |
| USB PD 3.0 | Up to 100W (20V @ 5A) | Same as 2.0, with improved communication protocol |
| USB PD 3.1 | Up to 240W (48V @ 5A) | Gaming laptops, workstations, high-performance devices |

#### Key Features:

- Extended Power Range (EPR): USB PD 3.1 introduces three new voltage
  levels: 28V (140W), 36V (180W), 48V (240W).

- Backward Compatibility: USB PD 3.1 devices negotiate power with the
  cable and charger - they will work with older USB PD chargers at lower
  wattages.

- Cable Requirements: To support the full 240W, cables must be rated for
  EPR (Extended Power Range) and clearly labeled as supporting USB PD
  3.1. Standard USB-C cables may only support up to 60W or 100W.

- Bidirectional Power: USB PD supports power flow in either direction -
  a laptop can both charge from a monitor and provide power to
  peripherals through the same cable.

#### Identification:

- Look for EPR or 240W markings on cables and chargers

- USB-IF certification logo indicates compliance with USB PD standards

- Cable e-marker chip stores capability information that devices can
  query

> **⚠️ Exam Trap:** Not all USB-C cables support USB Power Delivery, and
> even among PD cables, wattage ratings vary. A standard USB-C cable
> might only support 60W, while EPR cables are required for the full
> 240W capability. Always check cable specifications when
> troubleshooting charging issues with high-power devices.
>
> **💡 Pro Tip:** USB PD negotiation happens automatically - if a device
> isn’t charging at the expected rate, the issue is usually an
> incompatible cable or charger, not a device setting. Check the cable
> rating first.

## 🎥 Video Cables & Adapters

| Cable / Port | Standard | Notes |
|----|----|----|
| HDMI | Most common digital port. | Manages both video and audio over a 19-pin connector. Comes in Standard, Mini, and Micro sizes. |
| DisplayPort (DP) | Replacement for VGA/DVI. | Uses a 20-pin latching connector. Can transmit over USB-C via alt mode. |
| DVI | Older digital video port. | DVI-I carries both digital and analog; DVI-D is digital only. |
| VGA | Obsolete analog-only standard. | Uses a 15-pin D-type connector. Does not support audio. |

## 🔌 Hard Drive Cabling Standards

| Standard | Full Name | Speed | Connector | Key Details |
|----|----|----|----|----|
| IDE / PATA | Integrated Drive Electronics / Parallel ATA | Up to 133 MB/s | 40-pin wide ribbon cable | Legacy. Supports 2 drives per cable (master/slave jumper setting). Obsolete in modern systems. |
| SATA | Serial ATA | Up to 600 MB/s (SATA III) | 7-pin data + 15-pin power (L-shaped) | Modern standard for consumer HDDs and SSDs. One drive per cable. Hot-swappable on supported systems. |
| SAS | Serial Attached SCSI | Up to 22.5 Gb/s | SAS connector (similar shape to SATA, not cross-compatible) | Enterprise/server standard. Supports longer cable runs, higher reliability, and drive redundancy. |

> **⚠️ Exam Trap:** SAS controllers can accept SATA drives, but SATA
> controllers cannot accept SAS drives. The compatibility is
> one-directional.

# 🖨️ Module: Printers & Multifunction Devices

## 🎯 Module Objectives

By the end of this module, learners will be able to:

- Understand how laser printing works.

- Compare laser, inkjet, thermal, impact, and 3D printing technologies.

- Configure printers on wired, wireless, and cloud networks.

- Troubleshoot common printer failures and error symptoms.

## 🖨️ Laser Printers

Laser printers use an electro-photographic (EP) process to produce
high-quality text and graphics. They are common in business environments
due to speed and low cost per page.

### 🔧 Laser Printer Components

| Component                        | Purpose                         |
|----------------------------------|---------------------------------|
| Imaging Drum                     | Holds the electrostatic image   |
| Primary Corona / Charging Roller | Charges the drum                |
| Laser Assembly                   | Writes the image                |
| Developer Unit                   | Applies toner to the drum       |
| Transfer Roller/Belt             | Moves toner to paper            |
| Fuser Assembly                   | Melts toner into paper          |
| Pickup Rollers                   | Pull paper from tray            |
| Toner Cartridge                  | Supplies toner + sometimes drum |

### 🚨 Laser Printer Troubleshooting

| Symptom | Likely Cause | Fix |
|----|----|----|
| Streaks / vertical lines | Dirty corona, scratched drum | Replace toner/drum |
| Ghosted images | Bad fuser | Replace fuser assembly |
| Toner not binding | Fuser failure | Replace fuser |
| Paper jams | Worn rollers | Replace rollers / separation pad |
| Blank pages | Failed laser unit | Replace laser assembly |

### 🧮 Printer Languages

**PCL** vs. **PostScrip**t — Two competing page description languages
that tell a printer how to render a document. PCL (Printer Command
Language) is faster and more common for everyday business printing.
PostScript offers more precise, resolution-independent rendering and is
preferred for graphics and publishing work.

<img src="media/image28.png" style="width:6.375in;height:5.31318in"
alt="Laser electrophotographic printing process 7-step diagram with mnemonic &#39;Please Charge Everyone During Transfer Fusing Carefully&#39; and a troubleshooting reference table." />

### ⚙️ Laser Printing Process Steps

- Processing: The printer receives and rasterizes the page into a bitmap
  that the engine can print.

- Charging: A primary corona or charging roller applies a uniform
  negative charge to the drum.

- Exposing: A laser beam writes the image onto the drum by neutralizing
  selected areas.

- Developing: Negatively charged toner is attracted to neutral areas on
  the drum.

- Transferring: A transfer roller applies a positive charge to pull
  toner from the drum onto the paper.

- Fusing: The toner is melted and pressed into the paper using heat and
  pressure rollers.

- Cleaning: A cleaning blade removes any excess toner left on the drum
  after the transfer, and an erase lamp neutralizes any remaining
  electrical charge on drum.

> **⚠️ Exam Trap:** CompTIA heavily tests the exact chronological order
> of the laser printing cycle. The exam will occasionally include
> **Cleaning** as the final step after Fusing, where the photosensitive
> drum is scraped clean of residual toner before the cycle restarts!

## 🌈 Inkjet Printers

Inkjet printers spray microscopic droplets of ink onto paper using
Thermal bubble technology (HP, Canon) or Piezoelectric technology
(Epson).

### 🚨 Inkjet Troubleshooting

| Symptom                  | Cause              | Fix                       |
|--------------------------|--------------------|---------------------------|
| Streaks / missing colors | Clogged nozzles    | Run print head cleaning   |
| Faded print              | Low ink            | Replace cartridges        |
| Paper not feeding        | Dirty/worn rollers | Clean or replace rollers  |
| Colors incorrect         | Misalignment       | Run alignment utility     |
| Smearing                 | Wet ink, humidity  | Adjust paper type setting |

## 🔥 Thermal Printers

Used in cash registers, shipping centers, and kiosks. No ink or toner —
uses heat-sensitive paper. Extremely low maintenance.

### 🚨 Thermal Troubleshooting

| Symptom | Cause | Fix |
|----|----|----|
| Faded or blank prints | Dirty heating element | Clean the heating element with isopropyl alcohol. |
| Blank prints | Paper inserted upside down | Ensure the paper is inserted correctly (heat-sensitive side facing the element). |

## ⌨️ Impact Printers (Dot-Matrix)

Still used where multi-part (carbon copy) forms are required. Pins
strike ink ribbon. Very loud but durable and long-lasting.

### 🚨 Impact Troubleshooting

| Symptom | Cause | Fix |
|----|----|----|
| Light/faded text | Worn ink ribbon | Replace the faded ink ribbon. |
| Missing lines/gaps | Stuck or broken pins | Clean or replace the printhead. |
| Paper jams | Misaligned paper feed | Adjust the tractor feed mechanism. |

## 🧱 3D Printers

Uses additive manufacturing to build objects layer-by-layer. Common
technologies: FDM (Fused Deposition Modeling), SLA (Stereolithography),
SLS (Selective Laser Sintering).

### 🚨 3D Printer Troubleshooting

| Symptom | Cause | Fix |
|----|----|----|
| Clogged nozzle | Filament buildup/blockage | Clean or replace the extruder tip. |
| Poor bed adhesion | Unlevel bed or lack of adhesion | Re-level the print bed or apply an adhesive. |
| Filament snapping | Moisture-ruined filament or incorrect temperature settings | Check filament storage and adjust heat settings. |

## 🔮Virtual Printers

Virtual printers do not output physical paper. Instead, they act like
standard printers but send the final output to a digital file. This is
highly tested on the A+ exam as a method for preserving documents or
sharing them across platforms.

**Print to PDF:** Converts the document into a Portable Document Format
(.pdf). This is the industry standard for sharing documents that look
identical on any operating system.

**Print to XPS:** Microsoft’s native alternative to PDF. Creates an XML
Paper Specification (.xps) file. While less common today, it is still
tested on the exam.

**Print to Image:** Renders the document as an image file (e.g., .jpg,
.png, .tiff). Useful for graphic design workflows or when inserting a
document into a presentation.

**Print to File:** Saves the print output as a file (e.g., .prn or .ps)
that can be sent to a printer later without needing the original
application installed. Useful for printing from machines without a
direct printer connection.

> **⚠️ Exam Trap:** “Print spooler” questions are very common. If print
> jobs disappear from the queue but nothing prints, or the queue won’t
> clear, the fix is almost always: stop the Print Spooler service,
> delete the contents of the spool folder, restart the service.

### 

### 🔧 Virtual Printer Troubleshooting

| Symptom | Likely Cause | Fix |
|----|----|----|
| “Print to PDF” produces a blank or corrupted file | Driver conflict or insufficient disk space for temp file | Update/reinstall virtual printer driver; check free space |
| Print to File job won’t replay on real printer | Wrong printer driver used when generating the file | Re-capture using the driver that matches the destination printer |

<img src="media/image29.png" style="width:6.49306in;height:2in"
alt="Printer types reference graphic showcasing Inkjet, Thermal, Laser, Impact, and 3D FDM printers with exam-focused subtext for each." />

🖨️ Printer Networking & Management Tools

Bridging the gap between physical hardware and network infrastructure,
this table maps out the critical connectivity protocols and OS
management tools necessary to solve cross-domain printing issues on both
A+ exams.

#### Printer Networking & Configuration

- **Connections:** USB, Ethernet, Wi-Fi / Wi-Fi Direct, Bluetooth.

- **Protocols:** AirPrint (Apple), Cloud Printing, SMB, IPP, LPR.

- **Common Troubleshooting:**

  - Device not found (AirPrint/Wi-Fi)—Ensure the device and the printer
    are on the exact same subnet/VLAN.

  - Constantly dropping offline—Assign a static IP address or DHCP
    reservation to the printer.

#### Print Management Tools

- **Windows**: Print Management Console.

- **macOS**: System Settings → Printers & Scanners**.**

- **Network:** Embedded Web Server (EWS).

- **Common Troubleshooting:**

  - Stuck print queue—Stop the local Print Spooler service, clear the
    spool folder, and restart the service.

## Quick Review — Storage & Peripherals

### Questions

1.  A technician installs a 4 TB hard drive but only 2 TB of space is
    accessible. What is the most likely cause, and how is it fixed?

2.  A user receives a “File is too large for the destination file
    system” error when copying a 6 GB video file to a USB drive that has
    32 GB of free space. What is the problem and what are two possible
    solutions?

3.  What is the key difference between single-mode and multimode fiber
    optic cables?

4.  List the seven steps of the laser printing process in correct order.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### Answer Key

1.  The drive is using an MBR partition table, which has a maximum
    addressable size of 2 TB. The fix is to convert the drive to GPT
    (which supports drives up to 9.4 ZB) using Disk Management or
    diskpart.

2.  The USB drive is formatted with FAT32, which has a maximum file size
    limit of 4 GB. Solutions: (1) Reformat the drive to exFAT (best for
    cross-platform removable media with large files), or (2) Reformat to
    NTFS (if Windows-only use is acceptable).

3.  Single-mode fiber uses a single ray of light through a smaller core
    and is used for longer distances (but requires more expensive
    transceivers). Multimode fiber uses multiple light beams through a
    larger core and is used for shorter distances with less expensive
    transceivers.

4.  Processing → Charging → Exposing → Developing → Transferring →
    Fusing → Cleaning.

# 🌐 Module: Embedded Systems and IoT

## 🎯 Module Objectives

By the end of this module, learners will be able to:

- Define embedded systems and explain how they differ from
  general-purpose computers.
- Identify common IoT device categories in home, office, and industrial
  environments.
- Understand connectivity protocols used by IoT devices (Zigbee, Z-Wave,
  Matter, Thread).
- Recognize security risks unique to IoT and apply best practices for
  securing them.
- Troubleshoot common IoT connectivity issues.

## 🧠 What is an Embedded System?

An embedded system is a purpose-built computer designed for a specific,
dedicated function within a larger device. Unlike a general-purpose PC,
you cannot install arbitrary software on it.

| Feature | Embedded System | General-Purpose PC |
|----|----|----|
| Purpose | Single dedicated function | Any user-installed software |
| OS | Specialized / RTOS / embedded Linux | Windows, macOS, full Linux |
| User Interface | Minimal or none | Full GUI |
| Hardware Upgrades | Not possible | RAM, storage, GPU upgradeable |
| Software Install | Firmware updates only | Users install/remove freely |
| Real-Time Processing | Often required (must respond within strict timing) | Not typically required |
| Power Consumption | Low | Higher |
| Cost | Low (mass-produced, minimal components) | Higher (general-purpose hardware) |
| Lifespan | Long (years, often unchanged) | Shorter (regularly upgraded/replaced) |
| Examples | Smart thermostat, printer controller, car ECU | Desktop, laptop, tablet |

## 🌐 What is IoT?

The **Internet of Things (IoT)** refers to everyday physical devices —
embedded with sensors, software, and connectivity — that collect data,
communicate over a network, and often act on that data automatically. An
IoT device is essentially an embedded system (see above) with network
connectivity added.

IoT devices sense their environment, connect via
Wi-Fi/Bluetooth/Zigbee/cellular/etc., and often act automatically (e.g.,
a thermostat adjusting temperature) or alert a user (e.g., a doorbell
notification).

### Key characteristics of IoT devices:

- **Sensing** — collect data from the physical world (temperature,
  motion, light, sound, location)

- **Connectivity** — communicate over Wi-Fi, Bluetooth, Zigbee,
  cellular, or other protocols (see IoT Connectivity Protocols below)

- **Processing** — some analyze data locally; many send data to the
  cloud for processing

- **Action** — many can act on data automatically (e.g., a smart
  thermostat adjusting temperature) or notify a user (e.g., a doorbell
  sending an alert)

**ℹ️ Exam Tip:** IoT is a category, not a single technology. The exam
expects you to recognize IoT *devices* and their networking/security
implications — not a formal definition.

## 📡 IoT Device Categories

**Smart Home / Consumer:** Smart speakers, thermostats, smart lighting ,
smart locks, cameras/doorbells (Ring), smart appliances, robot vacuums,
smart home hub and Voice assistants (Alexa, Google Assistant, Siri)

**Office / Enterprise:** Network printers, IP cameras (PoE), VoIP
phones, badge/access control systems, environmental sensors, digital
signage, conference room systems.

**Industrial (IIoT):** SCADA systems (critical infrastructure), PLCs
(factory automation), medical devices (patient monitors), POS systems
(payment terminals), building automation (HVAC).

## 📶 IoT Connectivity Protocols

| Protocol | Range | Speed | Power | Use Cases |
|----|----|----|----|----|
| Wi-Fi | Medium (~50m) | High (Mbps) | High | Cameras, printers, anything needing bandwidth |
| Bluetooth/BLE | Short (10-100m) | Low-Med | Very Low | Wearables, sensors, locks, beacons |
| Zigbee | Short-Med (10-100m) | Low (250 Kbps) | Very Low | Smart lighting, home automation; mesh; needs hub |
| Z-Wave | Short-Med (30-100m) | Low (100 Kbps) | Very Low | Smart locks, thermostats; mesh; proprietary |
| NFC | Very Short (inches) | Low | Minimal | Tap-to-pay, door access badges |
| Cellular 4G/5G | Long (miles) | High | High | Fleet tracking, remote sensors |
| LoRaWAN | Very Long (miles) | Very Low | Very Low | Agriculture, city sensors, utility meters |
| Thread | Short-Med | Low | Very Low | Smart home (Matter standard); IPv6 mesh |
| Matter | Varies | Varies | Varies | Universal smart home standard (Apple, Google, Amazon) |

### 🔗 Thread Protocol

Thread is a low-power, IPv6-based mesh networking protocol designed
specifically for IoT (Internet of Things) devices in smart homes and
buildings.

#### What is Thread?

Thread is an open standard developed by the Thread Group (backed by
companies including Google, Apple, Amazon, and Samsung) to provide a
reliable, secure, and energy-efficient communication protocol for smart
home devices.

#### Key Characteristics:

| Feature | Description |
|----|----|
| Network Type | IPv6-based mesh network |
| Power Consumption | Low-power - designed for battery-operated devices |
| Range | Each device extends network range (mesh topology) |
| Security | Bank-level encryption (AES-128) with secure commissioning |
| Reliability | Self-healing mesh - if one device fails, traffic routes around it |
| Scalability | Supports hundreds of devices on a single network |

### How Thread Works:

**Mesh Topology**: Unlike traditional Wi-Fi (star topology where all
devices connect to a central router), Thread devices form a mesh where
each device can relay messages for others. This provides:

- **Extended range**: Signal hops from device to device

- **Reliability**: Multiple paths to destination

- **Self-healing**: Network automatically reroutes if a device fails

#### Device Roles:

1.  **Border Router**: Connects the Thread mesh network to other IP
    networks (Wi-Fi, Ethernet). Acts as the gateway. Typically a smart
    home hub or smart speaker.

2.  **Router**: Mains-powered devices (smart plugs, light switches) that
    relay messages for other devices.

3.  **End Device**: Battery-powered sensors and devices that sleep when
    not transmitting to conserve power.

### Thread vs. Other IoT Protocols:

| Protocol | Power Usage | Range | IP-Based | Security | Best Use Case |
|----|----|----|----|----|----|
| Thread | Low | Medium (mesh extends it) | Yes (IPv6) | High | Smart home devices, sensors |
| Zigbee | Low | Medium (mesh) | No | Medium | Legacy smart home devices |
| Z-Wave | Low | Medium (mesh) | No | Medium | Home automation, security |
| Wi-Fi | High | High | Yes | High | High-bandwidth devices |
| Bluetooth LE | Very Low | Short | No | Medium | Wearables, proximity devices |

### Matter and Thread:

**Matter** (formerly Project CHIP) is a smart home application layer
standard that can run over multiple network protocols, including Thread,
Wi-Fi, and Ethernet. Think of it this way:

- **Thread** = the network/transport layer (how devices communicate)

- **Matter** = the application layer (what they communicate)

Thread provides the reliable, low-power mesh network, while Matter
ensures devices from different manufacturers can understand each other.

**Benefits of Thread**:

1.  **No Single Point of Failure**: Mesh topology means the network
    continues functioning even if individual devices fail.

2.  **IPv6 Native**: Each device has its own IP address, enabling direct
    Internet connectivity and future-proofing.

3.  **Low Latency**: Optimized for responsive smart home actions (light
    switches, door locks).

4.  **Energy Efficient**: Battery-powered devices can last years on a
    single battery.

5.  **Secure by Design**: All communication is encrypted; devices are
    authenticated during commissioning.

**\**

**Requirements**:

- **Thread Border Router**: A device that bridges the Thread network to
  Wi-Fi/Ethernet (examples: Apple HomePod mini, Google Nest Hub, Amazon
  Echo 4th gen).

- **Thread-Compatible Devices**: Smart bulbs, sensors, locks,
  thermostats with Thread radios.

- **802.15.4 Radio**: Thread operates on the same 2.4 GHz frequency as
  Zigbee and uses the same IEEE 802.15.4 physical layer.

> **⚠️ Exam Trap:** Thread uses 2.4 GHz like Wi-Fi and Bluetooth, but it
> is NOT Wi-Fi. It uses a different physical layer (802.15.4) and forms
> mesh networks rather than connecting to an access point. Thread
> devices require a Thread Border Router to communicate with non-Thread
> devices.
>
> **💡 Pro Tip:** If a smart home device advertises “Matter over Thread”
> support, it means the device communicates using the Thread network
> protocol and speaks the Matter application language - providing both
> reliable mesh networking and cross-brand compatibility.

## 🔒 IoT Security Risks and Best Practices

**Why IoT devices are vulnerable:**

- Default credentials shipped unchanged (the \#1 IoT security risk)
- Infrequent or no firmware updates from manufacturers
- Cannot install antivirus or endpoint protection
- Always-on, always-connected (persistent attack surface)
- Weak or no encryption on some devices
- Difficult to monitor with traditional tools

**Best Practices:**

- Change all default credentials immediately during setup
- Isolate IoT on a separate network (guest VLAN or dedicated IoT
  network)
- Keep firmware updated; enable automatic updates where available
- Disable unused features (UPnP, remote access, cloud features)
- Use WPA3 or WPA2-AES; never use open networks for IoT
- Replace end-of-life devices that no longer receive security patches
- SCADA and industrial systems must be air-gapped from the internet

## 🛠️ IoT Troubleshooting

| Issue | Likely Cause | Fix |
|----|----|----|
| IoT device won’t connect to Wi-Fi | Wrong password, 5 GHz incompatibility (many IoT devices only support 2.4 GHz) | Verify password; check if device only supports 2.4 GHz; check MAC filtering |
| Device connected but unresponsive in app | Cloud service outage, hub/bridge offline | Check manufacturer status page; restart hub; power-cycle device |
| Smart home devices slow | Wi-Fi congestion, too many devices | Add dedicated IoT AP; change channel |
| Device disappears periodically | DHCP lease expiring, weak signal | Assign DHCP reservation; improve signal strength |
| Multiple devices stopped at same time | Router/hub failure, ISP outage | Check router; verify internet; power-cycle hub |
| Device stuck or update failing | Firmware update needed or interrupted | Check manufacturer website/app for new firmware; retry update with stable connection; avoid power loss during update |

## 💡 Exam Tips

- IoT devices should ALWAYS be on a separate network segment (guest or
  IoT VLAN).
- Default credentials are the \#1 IoT risk. If a compromised IoT device
  is mentioned, “change default password” is likely correct.
- Many IoT devices only support 2.4 GHz Wi-Fi. If a device won’t connect
  after router upgrade, check frequency band.
- SCADA and Industrial Control Systems should be air-gapped (never
  connected to internet).
- PoE is commonly used for IP cameras and VoIP phones (data + power on
  one cable).
- Cannot install antivirus on IoT. Network-level protections (firewalls,
  IDS/IPS, VLANs) are primary controls.

## Quick Review — IoT & Embedded Systems

### Questions

1.  Why should IoT devices be placed on a separate network segment, and
    what is the \#1 security risk associated with IoT devices?

2.  A smart thermostat connects to Wi-Fi but cannot communicate with the
    control app. The router is dual-band (2.4 GHz and 5 GHz). What is
    the most likely cause?

3.  What is the key difference between an embedded system and a
    general-purpose PC?

4.  A company has deployed 50 IP cameras using PoE. What does PoE
    provide, and what is the primary benefit?

### Answer Key

1.  IoT devices should be isolated because they cannot run antivirus or
    endpoint protection, receive infrequent updates, and are always
    connected — making them persistent attack surfaces. If compromised,
    isolation prevents lateral movement to critical systems. The \#1
    security risk is unchanged default credentials.
2.  Many IoT devices only support 2.4 GHz Wi-Fi and cannot connect to 5
    GHz networks. Verify the device’s Wi-Fi band support and ensure the
    router’s 2.4 GHz network is enabled and broadcasting.
3.  An embedded system is purpose-built for a single, dedicated function
    and cannot run arbitrary user-installed software. A general-purpose
    PC can run any compatible software the user chooses to install.
4.  PoE (Power over Ethernet) provides both data connection and
    electrical power over a single Ethernet cable. Primary benefit:
    eliminates the need for separate power outlets at each camera
    location, simplifying installation and reducing cost.

# CORE 2 - EXAM 220-1202

Operating Systems, Security, Troubleshooting & Operational Procedures

This section corresponds to the CORE 2 EXAM OBJECTIVES (220-1202) and
expands beyond hardware topics to address software functionality,
operating systems, security practices, and system-level troubleshooting.

# 🪟 Module: Windows Editions & Features

## 🎯 Module Objectives

The CompTIA A+ exam tests knowledge of the differences between Windows
editions - particularly which features are available in Home vs. Pro
vs. Enterprise. IT technicians must match the right edition to business
requirements.

By the end of this module, learners will be able to:

- Compare Windows 10/11 editions (Home, Pro, Pro for Workstations,
  Enterprise).

- Identify which features require Windows Pro or Enterprise.

- Understand upgrade paths between editions.

## 📊 Windows 10/11 Edition Comparison

| Feature                            | Home      | Pro             | Enterprise     |
|------------------------------------|-----------|-----------------|----------------|
| BitLocker Full Disk Encryption     | ❌        | ✅              | ✅             |
| Domain Join (Active Directory)     | ❌        | ✅              | ✅             |
| Group Policy Management            | ❌        | ✅              | ✅             |
| Remote Desktop (Host)              | ❌        | ✅              | ✅             |
| Hyper-V (Virtualization)           | ❌        | ✅              | ✅             |
| Windows Sandbox                    | ❌        | ✅              | ✅             |
| Assigned Access (Kiosk Mode)       | ❌        | ✅              | ✅             |
| AppLocker                          | ❌        | ❌              | ✅             |
| DirectAccess                       | ❌        | ❌              | ✅             |
| Long-Term Servicing Channel (LTSC) | ❌        | ❌              | ✅             |
| Windows Defender Credential Guard  | ❌        | ❌              | ✅             |
| Microsoft Store / Consumer Apps    | ✅        | ✅              | Optional       |
| Windows Update                     | ✅ (auto) | ✅ (controlled) | ✅ (WSUS/SCCM) |
| Max RAM Support                    | 128 GB    | 2 TB            | 6 TB           |
| Max CPU Sockets                    | 1         | 2               | 2              |

Home edition lacks all business features; Pro adds domain join,
BitLocker, and Hyper-V; Enterprise adds AppLocker and advanced security.

### 📝 Key Edition Decision Rules

- If a question mentions “joining a corporate domain” or “Active
  Directory” → requires Windows Pro or Enterprise (NOT Home).

- If a question mentions “BitLocker” → requires Windows Pro or
  Enterprise (NOT Home).

- If a question mentions “Group Policy” → requires Windows Pro or
  Enterprise (NOT Home).

- If a question mentions “Remote Desktop Host” (being connected TO, not
  connecting from) → requires Pro or Enterprise.

- If a question mentions “Hyper-V” → requires Pro or Enterprise with
  hardware virtualization enabled.

> **💡 Pro Tip:** Home edition = consumer. Pro = business. Enterprise =
