1.  A technician receives two tickets simultaneously: the CEO’s laptop
    won’t connect to Wi-Fi before a presentation in 10 minutes, and a
    warehouse employee wants a second monitor by next week. Using the
    Impact + Urgency = Priority formula, what priority level would each
    receive?

2.  A user reports “the printer is broken.” What should the technician
    ask first to identify the problem?

3.  An angry user says, “This computer is garbage! Nothing works!” Which
    response is most appropriate?

4.  What is the correct order of the 8-step ticket lifecycle?

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### Answer Key

1.  CEO ticket: Low Impact (one user) + High Urgency (time-sensitive) =
    P2/P3 (High/Medium priority). Warehouse ticket: Low Impact (one
    user) + Low Urgency (no time pressure) = P4 (Low priority). Handle
    the CEO ticket first.

2.  “What were you doing when the problem started?” or “Can you describe
    exactly what happens when you try to print?” — open-ended questions
    that gather symptoms without leading the user or making assumptions.

3.  “I understand this is frustrating. I’m here to help.” — Acknowledge
    the user’s frustration without being defensive, then offer a
    collaborative solution. Never say “calm down” or blame the user.

4.  Ticket Creation → Categorization & Prioritization → Troubleshooting
    & Documentation → Escalation (if needed) → Resolution &
    Implementation → Verification → User Education → Ticket Closure
    (closure is ALWAYS last, AFTER user confirms resolution).

# 🛠️ Module: Software Troubleshooting

## 🎯 Module Objectives

Software troubleshooting is 23% of the Core 2 exam - one of the largest
domains. Technicians must diagnose and resolve issues across operating
systems, applications, browsers, and mobile platforms.

By the end of this module, learners will be able to:

- Diagnose and resolve common Windows OS errors and performance issues.

- Troubleshoot application installation, update, and compatibility
  problems.

- Identify and resolve browser and internet application issues.

- Troubleshoot common mobile OS problems on iOS and Android.

- Address security-related software symptoms (malware, unauthorized
  access).

- Use appropriate tools to diagnose software-level failures.

## 🪟 Windows OS Troubleshooting

### 🚨 Common Windows Error Screens & Startup Problems

| Symptom / Error | Likely Cause | Recommended Fix |
|----|----|----|
| Blue Screen of Death (BSOD) | Driver failure, RAM error, corrupted system files, hardware fault. | Note the error code. Run memory diagnostic. Check Event Viewer for details. Roll back recent drivers. |
| “No Boot Device Found” / “No Operating System” | Corrupted bootloader, missing boot partition, failed drive. | Boot to Safe Mode. Run sfc /scannow. Check Windows Update history. Repair or roll back update. |
| System stuck at login screen / spinning circle | Corrupted Windows Update, profile corruption. | Boot to Safe Mode. Run sfc /scannow. Check Windows Update history. Repair or roll back update. |
| Slow startup | Too many startup programs, fragmented HDD, low RAM. | Task Manager → Startup tab. Run Disk Cleanup. Upgrade to SSD. Check for malware. |
| System randomly reboots / shuts down | Overheating, failing RAM, power supply issue. | Check CPU temps. Run Windows Memory Diagnostic. Inspect PSU and fans. |
| Black screen after login | Corrupted display driver, Remote Desktop conflict, explorer.exe crash. | Use Ctrl+Alt+Del. Open Task Manager. Run new task: explorer.exe. Update/roll back display driver. |

### 📈 Windows Performance Issues

| Performance Symptom | Diagnostic Steps & Fix |
|----|----|
| High CPU usage | Open Task Manager → Processes tab. Identify the process consuming CPU. Check for malware (high svchost.exe usage is a common malware sign). Disable unnecessary startup programs. |
| High RAM usage | Task Manager → Performance tab. Identify memory-hungry processes. Consider adding RAM. Check for memory leaks in applications. |
| High Disk usage (100%) | Common with HDDs and can cause severe slowdowns. Disable Superfetch/SysMain in Services. Check for malware. Consider replacing HDD with SSD. |
| App not responding (frozen) | Task Manager → right-click → End Task. If recurring, update or reinstall the application. Check Event Viewer for crash logs. |

## ⚙️ Application Troubleshooting

### 📦 Installation & Update Problems

| Issue | Likely Cause | Fix |
|----|----|----|
| Application fails to install | Insufficient disk space, missing dependencies, corrupt installer, permission issues. | Free disk space. Run installer as administrator. Download fresh installer. Check Windows Event Log. |
| Application won’t update | Corrupted update cache, network issue, insufficient permissions. | Clear application cache/temp files. Re-run update as admin. Uninstall and reinstall. |
| Application won’t start / immediately crashes | Corrupt app files, missing Visual C++ or .NET runtime, incompatible OS version. | Reinstall the application. Install required runtimes. Check compatibility settings (right-click → Properties → Compatibility). |
| Error: “DLL not found” / “Missing file” | Missing or corrupted dependency files. | Reinstall the application. Run sfc /scannow. Reinstall Visual C++ Redistributable or .NET Framework. |
| “Access Denied” during installation | UAC restrictions, insufficient permissions. | Right-click installer → “Run as administrator.” Verify user account has admin rights. |

### 🔄 Application Compatibility

When an application was designed for an older version of Windows and
fails to run correctly:

- Right-click the application → Properties → Compatibility tab.

- Enable “Run this program in compatibility mode for:” and select the
  appropriate OS version.

- Enable “Run this program as an administrator” if needed.

## 🌐 Browser & Internet Troubleshooting

When a user submits a ticket regarding browser issues, a technician must
quickly distinguish between a simple misconfiguration, a local privacy
misunderstanding, and an active attack. The table below maps common
symptoms to their root cause concepts and the correct fix.

| 🚨 Symptom | 🔍 Root Cause & Concept | 🛠️ Technician’s Fix |
|----|----|----|
| Browser won’t load pages (internet works) | Cache/Cookie Corruption or Proxy Misconfiguration. The browser has stale or corrupted local data, or traffic is being routed through a broken proxy. | Clear cache and cookies. Check proxy settings. Flush DNS (ipconfig /flushdns). Test in a different browser to isolate the issue. |
| Specific sites won’t load | DNS Resolution Failure or Hosts File Tampering. Malware commonly modifies the hosts file to redirect or block specific domains. Browser extensions can also interfere. | Check the hosts file (%SystemRoot%\System32\drivers\etc\hosts) for unauthorized entries. Try incognito mode. Disable extensions. Verify DNS settings. |
| Slow browser performance | Extension Bloat, Excessive Tabs, or Malware. Too many active extensions consume memory; malware may run background processes through the browser. | Disable unnecessary extensions. Update the browser. Run a malware scan. |
| “Your connection is not private” on every HTTPS site | Incorrect System Clock. SSL/TLS certificates rely on strict time validation. A clock set years in the past causes every modern certificate to appear mathematically invalid. | Verify and sync the OS date and time settings with an NTP server. This resolves the majority of widespread certificate errors immediately. |
| Certificate name mismatch warning | DNS Hijacking or On-Path Attack. The browser requested bank.com but the certificate returned identifies a different server — a serious red flag. | Do not bypass this warning. Flush the local DNS cache (ipconfig /flushdns). Check the router for unauthorized DNS server entries. |
| SSL/TLS certificate error — expired or untrusted CA | Expired Certificate or Self-Signed Certificate. The site’s certificate has lapsed, or it was issued by a CA not trusted by the browser (common on internal corporate tools). | Warn the user — do not dismiss on sensitive sites. For internal tools, the IT team may need to install the organization’s root CA certificate. |
| Browser homepage or search engine changed | Browser Hijacker. Malicious code has injected itself into the browser’s core configuration. | Run a full anti-malware scan. Perform a complete browser reset via browser settings to wipe all malicious modifications. Remove suspicious extensions. |
| Pop-up ads and redirects | Adware or Browser Hijacker Infection. Same root cause as homepage change — often installed as a bundle with freeware. | Remove suspicious extensions. Run an anti-malware scan. Reset browser settings to default. |
| Extension requesting unrelated permissions (e.g., a flashlight app asking for “access to all website data”) | Over-Privileged Extension. Malicious developers use simple utility tools as Trojan horses to harvest user data across every site visited. | Enforce the principle of least privilege. Remove any extension requesting permissions unrelated to its stated function. Only install extensions from official stores. |

> **💡 Pro Tip:** Wrong system clock makes every SSL certificate appear
> invalid so always check date/time **first** before diving deeper into
> certificate errors.\
> - Private/Incognito mode only prevents **local** history from being
> saved. It does not hide traffic from ISPs, employers, or network
> monitoring tools.\
> - Homepage change + redirects + pop-up ads can be a browser hijacker.
> The fix is always: malware scan + browser reset.\
> - A **certificate name mismatch** is not a routine error - it can
> indicate DNS hijacking. Treat it seriously, especially on financial or
> authentication sites.\
> - **Hosts file tampering** is a classic malware technique for blocking
> security sites or redirecting banking domains.

## 📟 Mobile OS Troubleshooting

### 📱 Common Mobile Software Issues

| Issue | Likely Cause | Fix |
|----|----|----|
| App crashes immediately | Corrupt app data, incompatible OS version, insufficient storage. | Clear app cache (Android: Settings → Apps → Storage). Update or reinstall app. Free storage space. |
| Device running slowly / freezing | Too many background apps, low storage, outdated OS. | Close background apps. Restart device. Free storage (delete unused apps/photos). Update OS. |
| Wi-Fi connects but no internet | IP conflict, DNS issue, router-side problem. | Forget the network and reconnect. Toggle Airplane Mode. Check if other devices can connect. |
| Mobile data not working | APN settings incorrect, SIM card issue, data limit reached. | Check APN settings (carrier-specific). Reinsert SIM. Check data plan usage. |
| Device won’t charge | Dirty/damaged charging port, faulty cable/adapter, battery issue. | Try different cable/adapter. Clean port with compressed air. Restart device. |
| Location services inaccurate | GPS off, poor signal, app permissions. | Enable High Accuracy mode. Ensure app has location permission. Toggle GPS off/on. |
| Touch screen unresponsive | Screen protector interference, software glitch, digitizer failure. | Restart device. Remove screen protector. Test with different input. May require hardware repair. |
| Cannot receive calls or texts | Airplane mode enabled, SIM issue, carrier outage. | Disable Airplane Mode. Reinsert SIM. Check carrier network status. |

### 🔁 Mobile OS Reset Options

When software issues cannot be resolved through normal troubleshooting,
a reset may be required:

| Reset Type | Description & When to Use |
|----|----|
| Soft Reset | Restarting or rebooting the device. Resolves minor glitches, freezes, and performance issues. No data loss. |
| Network Settings Reset | Resets all Wi-Fi, Bluetooth, cellular, and VPN settings to defaults. Use when network connectivity cannot be restored by normal means. |
| Factory Reset / Hard Reset | Erases all data and returns the device to factory settings. Use as a last resort for unfixable software issues or before selling/reassigning a device. ALWAYS back up data first. |

## 🔐 Security-Related Software Symptoms

Some software issues are symptoms of a security compromise rather than a
hardware or software defect. Technicians must recognize these patterns:

| Symptom | Possible Security Cause & Action |
|----|----|
| Antivirus suddenly disabled or uninstalled | Malware often disables AV to prevent detection. Boot from external media or Safe Mode to run AV scan. |
| New, unexpected user accounts appear | Possible backdoor or unauthorized account creation. Review accounts in Control Panel / Settings. Remove unknown accounts. |
| System files missing or altered | Malware, rootkit, or ransomware. Run sfc /scannow. Use anti-rootkit tools. Consider OS reinstall. |
| Pop-up warning saying “Your PC is infected – call this number” | Tech support scam (scareware). Do NOT call the number. Close browser via Task Manager if needed. Run legitimate AV scan. |
| Files encrypted with ransom message | Ransomware infection. Do NOT pay ransom. Disconnect from network immediately. Restore from backup. Report to management. |
| Slow performance with high disk/network activity (no user action) | Possible malware, cryptomining, or botnet activity. Check Task Manager and netstat. Run AV/malware scan. |

## 🧰 Software Troubleshooting Tools

| Tool | Purpose & Use Case |
|----|----|
| Task Manager | Monitor CPU/RAM/Disk/Network usage. Kill unresponsive processes. Manage startup programs. |
| Event Viewer | View application, system, and security logs for error codes and crash details. |
| Device Manager | Check for driver errors (yellow exclamation marks). Roll back or update drivers. |
| Reliability Monitor | Windows tool (in Control Panel) showing a visual timeline of system stability and recent failures. |
| Resource Monitor | Detailed view of CPU, memory, disk, and network usage per process. |
| sfc /scannow | Scans and repairs corrupted Windows system files. |
| DISM /Online /Cleanup-Image /RestoreHealth | Repairs the Windows image used by sfc. Run before sfc if system image is corrupted. |
| System Restore | Returns system settings to a previous restore point without affecting personal files. |
| msconfig (System Configuration) | Manage startup programs and services. Enable Selective or Diagnostic Startup to isolate issues. |
| Safe Mode | Boots Windows with only essential drivers. Used to diagnose whether the issue is caused by drivers or startup programs. |

# 🔧 Module: Hardware Troubleshooting

## 🎯 Module Objectives

By the end of this module, learners will be able to:

- Recognize common hardware failure symptoms.

- Diagnose and troubleshoot issues with desktops, laptops, components,
  and peripherals.

- Understand POST codes, BIOS/UEFI indicators, and error messages.

- Apply the CompTIA troubleshooting model systematically.

## 🧩 Common Hardware Symptoms

| Issue | Symptoms & Likely Causes | Fix |
|----|----|----|
| No Power / System Won’t Turn On | No fans, no lights, no display, no beeps. Causes: Failed PSU, bad power cable, motherboard short. | Test with known-good PSU. Reseat power connectors (ATX 24-pin, CPU 8-pin). Check surge protector or outlet. |
| POST Errors & Beep Codes | Causes: Bad RAM, missing CPU fan, no GPU detected, motherboard fault. | Reseat/replace RAM modules. Ensure CPU fan is connected. Install/reseat GPU. Consult motherboard manual for beep patterns. |
| No Display / Black Screen | Fans spin, lights on, no video output. Causes: Loose cable, GPU failure, RAM not seated, BIOS corruption. | Test with known-good monitor & cable. Reseat GPU and RAM. Clear CMOS (remove coin-cell battery 10-15 seconds). |
| Overheating | Sudden shutdowns, high fan speeds, thermal throttling. Causes: Dust buildup, failed fan, dried thermal paste. | Remove dust with compressed air. Replace thermal paste (clean old paste first). Improve airflow. |
| RAM Failures | Random reboots, blue screens, application crashes, failure to POST. | Test sticks individually. Run Windows Memory Diagnostic. Clean RAM contacts. Replace failing module. |
| Storage Failures | Slow performance, clicking sounds (HDD), boot device not found, file corruption. | Back up immediately. Replace failing drive. Check SSD firmware. Verify SATA/power cables. |
| Low IOPS | Sluggish multi-user or database performance even though raw throughput (MB/s) looks normal. Causes: drive can't keep up with many small simultaneous read/write requests. | Check IOPS rating vs. workload demand. Consider SSD/NVMe upgrade or RAID striping to increase available IOPS. |
| PSU Failures | Burning smell, intermittent shutdowns, random reboots, PSU fan not spinning. | Replace PSU (no internal repair). Ensure wattage is sufficient for CPU/GPU. |

## 🚨 Warning Signs of Hardware Failure

| Symptom               | Indicates                   |
|-----------------------|-----------------------------|
| Burning smell         | PSU or motherboard failure. |
| Clicking sounds       | HDD head crash.             |
| Bulging capacitors    | Motherboard or PSU issue.   |
| Frequent BSODs        | RAM or storage failure.     |
| Artifacting on screen | GPU overheating/failure.    |
| System clock resets   | Dead CMOS battery.          |

# 🔍 Module: Networking Troubleshooting

## 🎯 Module Objectives

By the end of this module, learners will be able to:

- Diagnose wired, wireless, and internet connectivity issues.

- Recognize symptoms such as APIPA addresses, DNS failures, slow speeds,
  and IP conflicts.

- Use network diagnostic tools (software + hardware).

- Support SOHO (Small Office/Home Office) networking environments.

## 🌐 Common Network Symptoms

| Symptom | Likely Causes | Fix |
|----|----|----|
| No Connectivity | “No Internet” message, Red X on network icon, cannot ping gateway. Causes: Unplugged cable, disabled adapter, bad port, router offline. | Check cable connection. Reset router. Enable adapter in OS. Test another port. |
| Limited or Intermittent Connectivity | Frequent disconnects, slow loading, Wi-Fi icon flickers. Causes: Weak signal, interference, congested channel, DHCP lease expiring. | Move closer to AP. Change Wi-Fi channel. Restart router. Renew IP address. |
| Port Flapping | A switch port repeatedly transitions up/down. Causes: failing cable, faulty NIC, duplex mismatch, loose connection. | Check switch logs. Replace cable. Test port with known-good device. Verify duplex/speed settings match on both ends. |
| APIPA Address (169.254.x.x) | Device did not receive DHCP IP - OS assigns a fallback self-assigned address. Causes: DHCP server offline, DHCP scope full, bad cable. | Restart DHCP server/router. Run ipconfig /release + ipconfig /renew. Reconnect to Wi-Fi with correct password. |
| DNS Issues (Can’t Resolve Names) | Can ping IP but not hostname. “DNS server not found.” Causes: DNS server down, incorrect settings, malware modifying DNS. | Flush DNS (ipconfig /flushdns). Set DNS to automatic or use public DNS (8.8.8.8). Check hosts file. Run malware scan. |
| Slow Performance on Network | Long loading, video buffering, high ping. Causes: Bandwidth saturation, Wi-Fi interference, faulty cables, ISP congestion. | Check for heavy downloads. Replace cable (Cat6 preferred). Move device/AP. Reset modem. |
| IP Address Conflicts | “IP address already in use,” random disconnects. Causes: Static IP duplicates DHCP address. | Change static IP. Use DHCP. Reserve IPs correctly in DHCP scope. |

## 🛠️ Diagnostic & Troubleshooting Tools

### 💻 Windows Command-Line Tools

| Command            | Purpose                           |
|--------------------|-----------------------------------|
| ipconfig           | Shows IP info; renew DHCP leases. |
| ping               | Tests connectivity to a host.     |
| tracert            | Traces route to destination.      |
| netstat            | Shows active network connections. |
| nslookup           | Tests DNS name resolution.        |
| ipconfig /flushdns | Clears the local DNS cache.       |

### **🔧** Essential Hardware Troubleshooting Tools

| Tool | Purpose |
|----|----|
| Multimeter | Test voltage, continuity, power rails. |
| Power Supply Tester | Quickly checks PSU health. |
| POST Card | Diagnoses motherboard POST errors. |
| Loopback Plug | Tests NIC transmit/receive functions. |
| Compressed Air | Removes dust blocking cooling systems. |
| Anti-static Wrist Strap & Mat | Prevents ESD damage. |
| Cable Tester | Checks continuity & wiring issues. |
| Tone Generator & Probe | Identifies cable runs in walls. |
| Crimper | Installs RJ-45 ends on UTP cables |
| Punch-down Tool | Terminates cables on patch panels. |
| Precision Screwdriver Set | Provides access to micro screws to use on modern PC components and cases. |

<img src="media/image35.png" style="width:6.375in;height:5.65in"
alt="An 11-panel reference grid titled &#39;Essential Hardware Troubleshooting Tools.&#39; It illustrates and defines the Multimeter, Tone Generator &amp; Probe, Precision Screwdriver Set, Compressed Air, Crimper, Loopback Plug, Punch-Down Tool, POST Card, Anti-Static Wrist Strap &amp; Mat, Cable Tester, and Power Supply Tester." />

## Quick Review — Domain Troubleshooting

### Questions

1.  A Windows system won’t boot after a driver installation. Using
    WinRE, what is the first recovery approach?

2.  A device receives a 169.254.x.x IP address. What does this indicate,
    and what command should the technician run to attempt resolution?

3.  A user reports their laptop “runs slow.” What should the technician
    check first in Task Manager?

4.  A legacy application won’t run on Windows 11. What is the first
    thing a technician should try before escalating?

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### Answer Key

1.  Boot into Safe Mode and roll back the driver via Device Manager. If
    that fails, use System Restore to return to a point before the
    driver was installed.

2.  It indicates that the device cannot reach a DHCP server (APIPA
    fallback address). Run ‘ipconfig /release’ followed by ‘ipconfig
    /renew’ to force a new DHCP request.

3.  Check the Processes tab to identify which process is consuming
    excessive CPU, RAM, or disk resources. High disk usage (100%) is a
    common cause of perceived slowness.

4.  Right-click the application → Properties → Compatibility tab →
    enable “Run this program in compatibility mode” and select the
    appropriate older Windows version. This resolves most legacy app
    issues.

# 📝 Module: Scripting Overview

## 🎯 Module Objectives

Scripting automates repetitive or complex tasks. Unlike compiled
programs, scripts are interpreted line-by-line, making them lightweight,
flexible, and ideal for system administration.

By the end of this module, learners will be able to:

- Identify and describe common scripting languages used in IT
  environments.

- Understand essential scripting concepts including variables, loops,
  conditionals, and functions.

- Troubleshoot common scripting errors including syntax issues,
  permissions, and missing modules.

## 🗣️ Common Scripting Languages

| Language | Purpose | File Type |
|----|----|----|
| PowerShell | Windows automation and administrative tasks. | .ps1 |
| Bash | Linux/macOS system and file operations. | .sh |
| Python | Cross-platform automation, parsing, and tooling. | .py |
| JavaScript | Browser automation and UI manipulation. | .js |
| Visual Basic Script | Legacy Windows automation. | .vbs |

## 🧩 Core Scripting Concepts

| Concept | Definition |
|----|----|
| Variables | Store data for use in scripts. |
| Conditionals | Allow scripts to perform different actions depending on the situation. |
| Loops | Repeat tasks automatically, ideal for system scans or file operations. |
| Functions | Reusable code blocks. |
| Arguments | Parameters for flexible input. |
| Comments | For documentation - exclude line from code (# // \<# \#\>). |
| Shebang | Defines the interpreter at the top of a script (e.g., \#!/bin/bash). |

## 🛡️ Security Considerations

Key principles when working with scripts:

- PowerShell Execution Policies: Restricted (no scripts run),
  RemoteSigned (local scripts run; downloaded must be signed),
  Unrestricted (not recommended).

- Linux/macOS Permissions: Scripts must be made executable: chmod +x
  script.sh

- Only run scripts from trusted sources. Avoid executing scripts
  delivered via email or chat.

- Follow Least Privilege: Run scripts with minimum necessary rights.
  Only elevate when required.

- Avoid Destructive Commands: Be cautious with rm -rf / (Linux) or del
  /s /q C: (Windows).

## 🚨 Common Scripting Errors

| Error Type | Symptom | Fix |
|----|----|----|
| Syntax Error | Script fails immediately with a parsing or unexpected token error. | Review the flagged line. Check for missing quotes, brackets, or incorrect operators. Run python -m py_compile script.py or pwsh -Command “Get-Command” to validate. |
| Permission Error | “Access denied,” “permission denied,” or script won’t execute at all. | Windows: Run PowerShell as administrator. Check execution policy: run Get-ExecutionPolicy. Set with Set-ExecutionPolicy RemoteSigned. Linux/macOS: Run chmod +x script.sh to make the file executable. Use sudo if elevated rights are needed. |
| Missing Module / Not Found | “Module not found,” “cannot find module,” or “command not recognized.” | Python: Install the missing module with pip install \<module-name\>. PowerShell: Install with Install-Module \<module-name\>. Bash: Install missing tools via apt-get install or yum install. |
| Wrong Interpreter | Script runs but produces unexpected output or fails with an unrecognized command error. | Verify the shebang line (#!/bin/bash, \#!/usr/bin/env python3). Confirm the correct interpreter is installed and in the system PATH. |
| Path Error | “File not found” even though the file exists. | Use absolute paths instead of relative paths. Verify the working directory with pwd (Linux) or cd (Windows). Check for typos in the path. |

Use the quick-reference chart to match an error message to its fix at a
glance — the table above walks through the “why,” this walks through
rapid pattern recognition for troubleshooting on the fly.

<img src="media/image36.png" style="width:6.51042in;height:7.24306in"
alt="Common scripting errors diagnosis guide, matching error messages to fixes for Syntax, Permission, Module, Interpreter, and Path-related issues." />

# 🗑️ Module: Data Destruction & Disposal

## 🎯 Module Objectives

Before disposing of, reselling, or repurposing any device, IT
technicians must ensure that sensitive data is completely and
unrecoverably destroyed. Improper disposal is one of the most common
sources of data breaches.

By the end of this module, learners will be able to:

- Identify the correct data destruction method for different media
  types.

- Differentiate between physical and software-based destruction methods.

- Understand regulatory and compliance requirements for data disposal.

- Explain what a Certificate of Destruction is and when it is required.

## 💾 Why Data Destruction Matters

Simply deleting files or even formatting a drive does NOT permanently
erase data. Standard deletion removes only the file pointer - the actual
data remains on the storage medium and can be recovered with forensic
tools. Proper destruction ensures data cannot be recovered by any means.

## 🔨 Physical Destruction Methods

Physical destruction is the most thorough and irreversible method. Used
when the device will not be reused.

| Method | Description & When to Use |
|----|----|
| Shredding | Industrial shredders physically destroy hard drives, SSDs, optical discs, and documents into tiny pieces. The gold standard for complete destruction. Often used with a Certificate of Destruction. |
| Drilling / Punching | Drilling holes through a hard drive platter or SSD destroys the storage media. Quick and effective for individual drives. Pieces may still be recoverable with advanced forensics. |
| Degaussing | Exposes magnetic storage (HDDs, magnetic tape) to a powerful magnetic field that scrambles all data. Effective for HDDs and tape but has NO effect on SSDs, flash memory, or optical discs (they have no magnetic media). |
| Incineration | Complete burning of media in a controlled furnace. Used for highly sensitive government/military data. Requires certified destruction facility. |
| Pulverizing | Crushing media into fine dust using industrial equipment. Similar to shredding - used for high-security environments. |

> **⚠️ Exam Trap:** Degaussing only works on magnetic media (HDDs,
> magnetic tape). It has absolutely NO effect on SSDs, USB drives,
> optical discs, or any flash-based storage.

### 💻 Software-Based Destruction Methods

Software methods overwrite data on the drive, making it unrecoverable
without physically destroying the media. Used when the device will be
reused, donated, or resold.

| Method | Description & Notes |
|----|----|
| Zero Fill (Single-Pass Overwrite) | Overwrites every bit on the drive with zeros. Fast and effective for modern drives. Recommended by NIST SP 800-88 for most situations. |
| Multi-Pass Overwrite (DoD 5220.22-M) | Overwrites data multiple times (3 or 7 passes) with different patterns. Once considered the gold standard but modern research shows single-pass is equally effective on current drives. |
| Secure Erase (ATA) | A built-in firmware command available on most modern SSDs and HDDs that resets all storage cells to factory state. Fast and highly effective - recommended for SSDs over multi-pass overwrite. |
| Cryptographic Erasure (Crypto Shredding) | Encrypts all data on the drive with a strong key, then destroys the key. The data is still present but permanently unreadable without the key. Very fast and effective. |
| Factory Reset | Restores a device to factory settings. For mobile devices with hardware encryption enabled, this effectively destroys the encryption key, rendering data unrecoverable. For non-encrypted devices, data may be partially recoverable. |

> **⚠️ Exam Trap:** Multi-pass overwrite (DoD method) is largely
> ineffective on SSDs due to wear-leveling - the drive’s firmware may
> skip sectors. Use Secure Erase or cryptographic erasure for SSDs
> instead.

<img src="media/image37.png" style="width:6.46875in;height:8.48854in"
alt="Data destruction and disposal methods matrix, categorizing physical vs. software-based methods and detailing media compatibility for compliance." />

## 📋 Certificate of Destruction

A Certificate of Destruction (CoD) is a formal document provided by a
certified destruction vendor confirming that specified media was
destroyed in compliance with relevant regulations. It includes:

- Description and serial numbers of the destroyed media.

- Date and method of destruction.

- Name and signature of the certified destruction provider.

- Chain of custody documentation.

Organizations required by regulations (HIPAA, GDPR, PCI-DSS, SOX) to
protect sensitive data must retain Certificates of Destruction as
evidence of compliance.

> **💡 Pro Tip:** Degaussing = magnetic destruction ONLY. For SSDs use
> Secure Erase or physical destruction. Certificate of Destruction is
> required when regulations (HIPAA, PCI-DSS, GDPR) mandate proof of
> disposal. Deleting files + emptying the recycle bin does NOT destroy
> data.

## 📋 Choosing the Right Method

| Scenario | Recommended Method | Why |
|----|----|----|
| Reselling a corporate laptop with an HDD | Multi-pass overwrite or Zero Fill | Drive will be reused — physical destruction not needed. Overwrite renders data unrecoverable. |
| Disposing of an old SSD from an HR workstation | Secure Erase or physical shredding | Multi-pass overwrite is unreliable on SSDs. Secure Erase or destruction is preferred. |
| Decommissioning a server with magnetic tape backups | Degaussing + shredding | Degaussing destroys tape data; shredding provides physical assurance. |
| Repurposing a company phone (encrypted) | Factory reset | Hardware encryption + factory reset destroys the key, making data unrecoverable. |
| Disposing of drives containing classified information | Physical shredding + Certificate of Destruction | Highest security requirement - physical destruction with documented chain of custody. |
| Retiring dozens of old laptops for charity donation | Zero fill overwrite | Cost-effective, allows reuse, meets standard compliance requirements. |

# ⚖️ Module: Licensing & Regulated Data

## 🎯 Module Objectives

IT technicians regularly deal with software licensing compliance and
must understand the types of data their organization handles and the
regulations that govern it. Mishandling either can expose the
organization to significant legal and financial risk.

By the end of this module, learners will be able to:

- Identify and describe common software license types.

- Explain the consequences of non-compliance with licensing terms.

- Define PII, PHI, PCI, and other regulated data categories.

- Describe basic requirements under GDPR, HIPAA, and PCI-DSS.

- Understand the IT technician’s role in data protection compliance.

## 💿 Software Licensing Types

| License Type | Description & Key Details |
|----|----|
| OEM (Original Equipment Manufacturer) | Software licensed to a specific piece of hardware. Cannot be transferred to a different device. Typically pre-installed by the manufacturer. Example: Windows that comes with a new PC. |
| Retail / Full Packaged Product | Purchased by a consumer and can be transferred to a new device (by uninstalling from the old one). More flexible than OEM. Example: Purchasing a boxed copy of software. |
| Volume License | A single license agreement covering multiple installations across an organization. Managed through a central portal. Cost-effective for businesses. Example: Microsoft Volume Licensing, Microsoft 365 Business. |
| Subscription | Recurring payment (monthly/annual) for ongoing access to software. Stops working when the subscription lapses. Includes automatic updates. Examples: Microsoft 365, Adobe Creative Cloud. |
| Open Source | Source code is publicly available. Use, modification, and distribution are governed by specific open-source licenses (GPL, MIT, Apache). Free but comes with obligations (e.g., GPL requires sharing modifications). |
| Freeware | Free to use but source code is not provided and modification/redistribution is restricted. Different from open source. Example: most free utility tools. |
| Shareware | Try-before-you-buy model. Functional version distributed freely for a trial period; full features require purchase. |
| End-User License Agreement (EULA) | The legal contract between the software vendor and the user defining how the software may be used. Must be accepted during installation. IT must ensure compliance. |

> **⚠️ Exam Trap:** OEM licenses are tied to the hardware - you cannot
> move them to a different machine. Retail licenses can be transferred.
> Volume licenses belong to the organization, not individual users.

## 🔒 Regulated Data Categories

Certain types of data are subject to legal regulations that impose
specific requirements for how they must be stored, accessed,
transmitted, and disposed of. IT technicians must recognize these
categories.

| Data Category | Definition & Regulatory Context |
|----|----|
| PII (Personally Identifiable Information) | Any data that can identify a specific individual: name, address, SSN, email, phone, date of birth, IP address, biometric data. Protected under multiple regulations including GDPR and many US state laws. |
| PHI (Protected Health Information) | Any health information that can identify a patient: diagnoses, treatment records, insurance information, test results. Regulated under HIPAA in the United States. |
| PCI DSS Data | Payment card data: cardholder names, card numbers, CVVs, expiration dates. Regulated under the Payment Card Industry Data Security Standard (PCI-DSS). |
| Intellectual Property (IP) | Proprietary business information: trade secrets, source code, product designs, business strategies. Protected under copyright, patent, and trade secret law. |
| FERPA Data | Student education records. Protected under the Family Educational Rights and Privacy Act in the United States. Applies to schools receiving federal funding. |
| Corporate Confidential | Internal business data not legally regulated but protected by employment contracts and NDA agreements: financial projections, acquisition plans, HR data. |

## 🌍 Key Regulations To Know

<table>
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 33%" />
<col style="width: 66%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th>Regulation</th>
<th>Key Requirements for IT</th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td><p>GDPR</p>
<p>(General Data Protection Regulation)</p></td>
<td>EU regulation governing PII of EU residents. Requires: explicit
consent for data collection, right to erasure (“right to be forgotten”),
data breach notification within 72 hours, data minimization. Applies to
any organization serving EU residents regardless of location.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p>HIPAA</p>
<p>(Health Insurance Portability and Accountability Act)</p></td>
<td>US law protecting PHI. Requires: access controls limiting PHI to
those who need it, audit logs of all PHI access, encryption of PHI in
transit and at rest, secure disposal of PHI media, Business Associate
Agreements with vendors.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p>PCI-DSS</p>
<p>(Payment Card Industry Data Security Standard)</p></td>
<td>Standard for organizations handling payment cards. Requires:
encrypted transmission of cardholder data, restricted access to card
data, regular security testing, not storing CVV codes after
authorization.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p>SOX</p>
<p>(Sarbanes-Oxley Act)</p></td>
<td>US law for public companies requiring accurate financial reporting
and IT controls around financial data systems. IT must maintain audit
trails and access controls for financial systems.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

## 🧑‍💻 IT Technician’s Role in Compliance

- Implement access controls ensuring only authorized personnel can
  access regulated data.

- Ensure devices handling regulated data use full-disk encryption.

- Follow proper data disposal procedures (see Data Destruction module)
  for media containing regulated data.

- Never share, copy, or access regulated data beyond what is needed for
  the job (principle of least privilege).

- Report suspected data breaches immediately - most regulations have
  strict breach notification deadlines.

- Ensure software licenses are current and compliant - unlicensed
  software creates legal liability.

### Consequences of Non-Compliance

Software vendor audits result in forced license purchases and fines.
Legal action and financial penalties. Loss of access (subscription
termination). Organizational reputation damage. Personal liability for
IT staff who knowingly allow unlicensed software.

# 📋 Module: Asset Management & Documentation

## 🎯 Module Objectives

Asset management and documentation are core operational responsibilities
for IT professionals. Proper documentation reduces troubleshooting time,
ensures compliance, and maintains organizational knowledge when staff
changes occur.

By the end of this module, learners will be able to:

- Explain the purpose and components of an IT asset management system.

- Identify and describe common types of IT documentation.

- Understand the importance of change management documentation.

- Create and maintain basic network and system documentation.

## 🏷️ Asset Management

IT asset management (ITAM) tracks hardware and software assets
throughout their entire lifecycle - from procurement and deployment
through maintenance and final disposal.

| Asset Management Component | Description |
|----|----|
| Asset Tag / Barcode | A physical label (barcode or QR code) applied to equipment for inventory tracking. Tied to a database record containing the device’s full details. |
| Asset Inventory Database | Central repository recording all hardware and software assets. Includes: make/model, serial number, purchase date, warranty, assigned user, location, and current status. |
| Procurement Records | Documentation of when and how assets were purchased, including vendor invoices, purchase orders, and licensing agreements. |
| Warranty & Support Records | Tracks warranty expiration dates, support contract terms, and vendor contact information for each asset. |
| Disposal Records | Documents how decommissioned assets were handled - sold, donated, recycled, or destroyed. Includes Certificates of Destruction where required. |
| Software Licensing | Tracks software licenses (number of seats, license type, renewal dates) to ensure compliance and avoid over/under-licensing. |
| CMDB (Configuration Management Database) | Centralized database tracking IT assets and their relationships. Supports change management and troubleshooting. |

## 📄 IT Documentation Types

| Document Type | Purpose & Contents |
|----|----|
| Network Diagram | Visual map of the network infrastructure showing devices (routers, switches, servers, APs), their connections, IP addresses, and VLANs. Essential for troubleshooting and planning. |
| Network Topology Map | Shows the physical and logical layout of the network. Logical topology shows data flow; physical topology shows actual cable and device placement. |
| Standard Operating Procedure (SOP) | Step-by-step instructions for routine IT tasks (e.g., onboarding a new user, deploying a workstation, responding to a security incident). Ensures consistency regardless of who performs the task. |
| Knowledge Base Article | A searchable document describing how to resolve a specific issue, configure a system, or understand a technology. Built from resolved tickets and technician experience. |
| Runbook / Playbook | Detailed procedures for specific operational or incident response scenarios. Similar to SOPs but often more granular and scenario-specific. |
| Change Log | A record of all changes made to systems, configurations, or infrastructure. Includes what changed, when, by whom, why, and the approval reference. |
| Incident Report | Documents a security incident or major outage from detection through resolution. Includes timeline, impact, root cause, and corrective actions taken. |
| Acceptable Use Policy (AUP) | Policy document defining what employees are and are not allowed to do with company IT resources. |
| Disaster Recovery Plan (DRP) | Documented procedures for restoring IT systems and operations following a catastrophic event (fire, flood, ransomware attack, etc.). |
| Business Continuity Plan (BCP) | Broader than a DRP - covers how the entire business continues operating during and after a disruption, not just IT systems. |

## 🔄 Change Management

Change management is the formal process for requesting, evaluating,
approving, implementing, and documenting changes to IT systems. It
prevents unplanned outages caused by untested changes.

| Step | CompTIA Term | Description |
|----|----|----|
| 1 | Documented Business Processes | The formal reason and business justification for the change is recorded. |
| 2 | Purpose of the Change | Clearly defines *why* the change is needed (new requirement, bug fix, security patch, etc.). |
| 3 | Scope of the Change | Defines what systems, users, or processes will be affected. |
| 4 | Risk Analysis | Evaluates potential risks and assigns a risk level (low/medium/high). |
| 5 | Plan for the Change | Documents exactly how the change will be implemented, including a timeline. |
| 6 | End-User Acceptance | Confirms affected users understand and accept the upcoming change. |
| 7 | Change Board Approval | The CAB reviews and formally approves the change request. |
| 8 | Backout Plan | Documents exactly how to revert the change if something goes wrong. |
| 9 | Document the Changes | The change log is updated with the full record after implementation. |

## 🧾 Additional Change Management Terms

| Term | Description |
|----|----|
| Change Freeze | Defined period where non-emergency changes are prohibited (e.g., peak business season). |
| Peer Review | Another technician reviews a planned change before implementation to catch missed risks. |
| Standard Change | Low-risk, pre-approved, repeatable change (e.g., routine patching) — no full CAB review needed. |
| Normal Change | Follows the full change process, including CAB review, due to higher risk/complexity. |
| Emergency Change | Urgent change to resolve a critical issue, often bypassing normal approval; documented after the fact. |

<img src="media/image38.png" style="width:6.37607in;height:4.13542in" />

> **💡 Pro Tip:** Network diagrams and documentation save enormous time
> during troubleshooting - especially in complex environments. - Change
> management prevents “it worked fine before” problems - always document
> changes. - Knowledge base articles are built from closed help desk
> tickets - good ticket documentation builds the KB. - Asset tags
> connect physical hardware to the inventory database - serial number +
> asset tag = full device history.

# 🤖 Module: Artificial Intelligence (AI) Basics

## 🎯 Module Objectives

Artificial Intelligence is a new addition to the CompTIA A+ V15 exam
objectives. IT technicians are expected to understand basic AI concepts,
how AI tools integrate into business workflows, the limitations of AI,
and the security and ethical considerations that come with AI use in the
workplace.

By the end of this module, learners will be able to:

- Define key AI concepts including machine learning, generative AI, and
  large language models.

- Differentiate between public AI models and private/organizational AI
  models.

- Identify appropriate and inappropriate use cases for AI tools in an IT
  environment.

- Recognize the limitations of AI including bias, hallucinations, and
  accuracy concerns.

- Understand basic AI security and privacy considerations.

## 🧠 Key AI Concepts

| Type | Definition & Relevance |
|----|----|
| Artificial Intelligence (AI) | The simulation of human intelligence in machines - enabling systems to perform tasks that typically require human thinking (learning, reasoning, problem-solving). |
| Machine Learning (ML) | A subset of AI where systems learn from data to improve performance without being explicitly programmed. The more data, the better the model learns. |
| Generative AI | AI that creates new content (text, images, code, audio) based on patterns learned from training data. Examples: ChatGPT, GitHub Copilot, DALL-E. |
| Large Language Model (LLM) | A type of generative AI trained on massive text datasets to understand and generate human-like text. Powers most modern AI chatbots and assistants. |
| Natural Language Processing (NLP) | AI ability to understand, interpret, and generate human language. Enables voice assistants, chatbots, and automated translation. |
| Neural Network | A computing system loosely inspired by the human brain. Layers of interconnected nodes process data to recognize patterns and make decisions. |
| Training Data | The dataset used to teach an AI model. The quality and diversity of training data directly affects model accuracy and bias. |

## 🏢 Public vs. Private AI Models

A critical distinction for IT professionals is understanding the
difference between publicly accessible AI tools and private
organizational AI deployments - particularly from a data security
perspective.

| Public AI Models | Private / Organizational AI Models |
|----|----|
| Accessible to anyone via the internet (e.g., ChatGPT, Claude, Gemini). | Deployed internally by an organization, not accessible outside the company. |
| Data entered may be used to train future models. | Data stays within the organization’s controlled environment. |
| Lower cost - often free or subscription-based. | Higher cost - requires infrastructure and maintenance. |
| Risk: Employees may accidentally share confidential data. | Designed to protect sensitive business and customer data. |
| Examples: ChatGPT (OpenAI), Copilot (Microsoft), Gemini (Google). | Examples: Azure OpenAI Service (private deployment), on-premises LLMs. |

> **⚠️ Exam Trap:** Entering sensitive company data, customer PII, or
> trade secrets into a public AI tool is a security violation.
> Organizations must have clear AI use policies that employees follow.

## ✅ Appropriate Use Cases for AI in IT

- Drafting documentation, knowledge base articles, and SOPs.

- Summarizing long technical documents or logs.

- Writing and reviewing code or scripts (with human review).

- Generating test cases and troubleshooting checklists.

- Answering general technical questions and providing guidance.

- Automating repetitive tasks like ticket categorization and report
  generation.

## 🚫 Inappropriate / Risky Use Cases

- Entering customer PII, passwords, or financial data into a public AI
  tool.

- Using AI-generated code in production without thorough human review.

- Relying on AI for compliance decisions without expert verification.

- Sharing internal network diagrams, system configurations, or security
  policies.

- Using AI output as a final answer for medical, legal, or
  safety-critical decisions.

## ⚠️ AI Limitations To Understand

| Limitation | Explanation & Impact |
|----|----|
| Hallucinations | AI confidently generates information that is factually incorrect or completely fabricated. Always verify AI output, especially for technical facts, port numbers, or procedures. |
| Training Data Cutoff | AI models are trained on data up to a certain date and have no knowledge of events or products released after that point. |
| Bias | AI can reflect biases present in its training data - leading to unfair, inaccurate, or skewed outputs, especially in areas involving people or demographics. |
| Context Limitations | AI models can only process a limited amount of text at once (context window). Very long documents may be summarized inaccurately if they exceed this limit. |
| No Real-World Verification | AI cannot independently verify claims against live systems, databases, or the internet (unless given tools to do so). |
| Privacy Risk | Data entered into public AI tools may be retained and used for training. Sensitive data should never be entered into public AI systems. |

## 🔐 AI Security Considerations

- Prompt Injection: Attackers craft malicious inputs designed to
  override an AI’s instructions and make it behave unexpectedly. A
  significant security concern for AI-integrated applications.

- Data Leakage: Public AI tools may expose confidential data shared in
  prompts. Use private/organizational AI deployments for sensitive
  workflows.

- Model Poisoning: In ML pipelines, attackers can corrupt training data
  to cause a model to make incorrect decisions. A concern for
  organizations training their own models.

- Deepfakes: AI-generated realistic fake audio/video of real people.
  Used in social engineering attacks (e.g., fake CEO video call
  requesting a wire transfer).

- Plagiarism — A key AI policy concern where AI-generated content is
  presented as original human work without disclosure. Organizations
  should have clear policies on when and how AI-assisted content must be
  disclosed or attributed.

## 🏢 Organizational AI Policies

IT technicians must understand and enforce their organization’s AI
acceptable use policy. Key elements typically include:

- Which AI tools are approved for use (whitelist of approved platforms).

- What types of data may and may not be entered into AI systems.

- Requirements for human review of AI-generated content before use.

- Reporting procedures for AI-related security incidents.

- Copyright and intellectual property considerations for AI-generated
  work.

> **💡 Pro Tip:** Know the difference between public AI
> (internet-accessible, data privacy risk) and private AI
> (organizational control, data stays internal). - Hallucination = AI
> confidently making things up. Always verify AI output. - AI cannot
> replace human judgment for compliance, legal, medical, or safety
> decisions. - Entering sensitive data into a public AI tool = potential
> security incident. Know your org’s AI use policy.

## Quick Review — Compliance & IT Responsibilities

### Questions

1.  A technician needs to securely erase an SSD containing sensitive
    employee data. The drive will be reused. Why is multi-pass overwrite
    NOT appropriate, and what method should be used instead?

2.  What is the difference between an OEM license and a Retail license
    in terms of transferability?

3.  What is the purpose of a Change Advisory Board (CAB) in the change
    management process?

4.  An employee enters customer credit card numbers into a public AI
    chatbot for analysis. What is the primary security and compliance
    concern?

5.  What must accompany destroyed media containing regulated data (PHI,
    PCI data) to prove compliance?

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### Answer Key

1.  Multi-pass overwrite is unreliable on SSDs because wear-leveling
    causes the firmware to skip sectors, leaving data intact. Use the
    Secure Erase (ATA firmware command) instead, which resets all
    storage cells at the firmware level. Alternatively, use
    cryptographic erasure if the drive was encrypted.

2.  OEM licenses are tied to the original hardware and cannot be
    transferred to a different machine. Retail licenses can be
    transferred to a new device by uninstalling from the old one.

3.  The CAB is a group of stakeholders that reviews, evaluates risk, and
    approves or rejects change requests before implementation. It
    prevents untested changes from causing unplanned outages.

4.  Data entered into public AI tools may be retained and used for model
    training, violating PCI-DSS requirements that prohibit sharing
    cardholder data with unauthorized third parties. This constitutes a
    potential data breach and compliance violation. Always use
    private/organizational AI for sensitive data.

5.  \* A Certificate of Destruction — formal documentation from a
    certified destruction vendor confirming that specified media was
    destroyed in compliance with relevant regulations. Required for
    HIPAA, PCI-DSS, GDPR, and other regulated data types.

# 📝 EXAM PREPARATION & STUDY TOOLS

This section brings together everything you need for final exam
preparation: mnemonics for every domain, exam traps, quick reference
cards and diagrams, a practice test covering all modules, a 8-week study
plan, and 500 term Glossary.

## 🧠 Mnemonics and Reference Cards

### 📧 Email Protocols

**Memory hook:** *“**Send It, Pop It, Map It**” - SMTP sends out, POP3
pops down to one device, IMAP maps across all devices*

| Port | Protocol | TCP/UDP | Description |
|----|----|----|----|
| 25 / 587 | SMTP | TCP | Sends outbound email. Port 25 = server-to-server. Port 587 = client submission with STARTTLS. Always use 587 for client config. |
| 110 / 995 | POP3 / POP3S | TCP | Downloads email to one device and removes from server. Port 110 = plain. Port 995 = encrypted (SSL/TLS). Not recommended for multi-device users. |
| 143 / 993 | IMAP / IMAPS | TCP | Keeps email on server and syncs across all devices. Port 143 = plain. Port 993 = encrypted (SSL/TLS). Recommended for modern use. |

Quirky Memory Hooks:

- **“S**end **M**ore **T**asty **P**izza” 🍕 (**SMTP** = Sends outbound)

- **POP3** is like popping a balloon - it’s **one and done** (downloads
  to one device and destroys the server copy).

- **IMAP** is like a **map** on your phone - no matter where you travel
  or what device you look at, the view stays completely synchronized.

### 🔄 The 6-Step CompTIA Troubleshooting Process: IETEVD

| Step | Action |
|----|----|
| 1 - I | Identify the Problem: Gather information, question the user, identify recent changes, and check documentation. |
| 2 - E | Establish a Theory of Probable Cause: Question the obvious, consider multiple approaches, and research based on the symptoms. |
| 3 - T | Test the Theory: Confirm your theory and verify the root cause. (If the theory is incorrect, loop back and re-establish a new one). |
| 4 - E | Establish a Plan of Action: Plan the fix, notify affected users, consider impacts, and implement the solution. |
| 5 - V | Verify Full System Functionality: Confirm the resolution works and implement preventive measures to stop the issue from recurring. |
| 6 - D | Document Findings: Record the symptoms, root cause, steps taken, resolution, and preventive measures for the knowledge base. |

### 🗂 The I-E-T-E-V-D Mnemonic Matrix

| Style | Variation 1 | Variation 2 |
|----|----|----|
| Professional | I Expect To Execute Very Decisively | Investigate Every Technical Error Very Deliberately |
| Easy to Recall | I Eat Tacos Every Valentine’s Day | Impatient Elephants Trample Every Village Daily |
| Quirky / Memorable | Iguanas Enjoy Teasing Earthworms Very Deliberately | I Enjoy Taming Energetic Velociraptors Diligently |

<img src="media/image39.png" style="width:6.6875in;height:8.65088in"
alt="A six-step flowchart illustrating the CompTIA Troubleshooting Methodology mnemonic I E T E V D. The steps are sequentially listed as: 1. Identify the problem; 2. Establish a theory of probable cause; 3. Test the theory to determine cause; 4. Establish a plan of action to resolve the problem and implement the solution; 5. Verify full system functionality and, if applicable, implement preventive measures; 6. Document findings, actions, and outcomes. " />

### 🌐 Web Browsing

**Memory hook:** *“80 is plain, 443 is protected” - just two numbers, no
phrase needed*

| Port | Protocol | TCP/UDP | Description |
|----|----|----|----|
| 80 | HTTP | TCP | Hypertext Transfer Protocol. Unencrypted web browsing. Never use for sensitive data - traffic can be intercepted. |
| 443 | HTTPS | TCP | HTTP Secure. Encrypted web browsing using TLS/SSL. Lock icon in browser. Standard for all modern websites. |

Quirky Memory Hooks:

- **80** mph in a **plain** sedan vs. **443** layers of **armor** on a
  **Secured** bank vault.

  - Alternatively: Imagine an **80-year-old** grandmother shouting her
    password out loud on the street (**HTTP - Unencrypted**), versus a
    secret agent with **443 gadgets** locking down a briefcase
    (**HTTPS - Secure**).

### 📶 Wi-Fi Standards Quick Reference

| Standard | Name | Max Speed | Key Feature |
|----|----|----|----|
| 802.11a | — | 54 Mbps | 5 GHz only. Legacy. |
| 802.11b | — | 11 Mbps | 2.4 GHz. Legacy. |
| 802.11g | — | 54 Mbps | 2.4 GHz. Legacy. |
| 802.11n | Wi-Fi 4 | 600 Mbps | 2.4 & 5 GHz. MIMO introduced. |
| 802.11ac | Wi-Fi 5 | 3.5 Gbps | 5 GHz only. MU-MIMO. |
| 802.11ax | Wi-Fi 6 | 9.6 Gbps | 2.4/5 OFDMA. Best for dense environments. |
| 802.11ax (6E) | Wi-Fi 6E | 9.6 Gbps | 6 GHz extension. Requires 6E-specific hardware. |
| 802.11be | Wi-Fi 7 | 46 Gbps | Multi-Link Operation (MLO). Latest standard. |

<img src="media/image40.png" style="width:6.76095in;height:5.88299in"
alt="Modern connectivity standards reference chart comparing Wi-Fi generations by speed and frequency." />

### 🌐 Crucial IP Addresses Quick Reference

| Address / Range | Type | What it Means on the Exam |
|----|----|----|
| 169.254.x.x | APIPA | The device cannot reach the DHCP server. |
| 127.0.0.1 | IPv4 Loopback | Used to ping the local network card to test if TCP/IP is working. |
| ::1 | IPv6 Loopback | The IPv6 equivalent of 127.0.0.1. |
| 10.x.x.x | Class A Private | A non-routable private IP (used heavily in corporate networks). |
| 192.168.x.x | Class C Private | A non-routable private IP (used heavily in home/SOHO networks). |

### 🔐 Security Protocols Quick Reference

| Protocol / Feature | Key Detail |
|----|----|
| WEP | Broken - cracks in minutes. Never use. |
| WPA (TKIP) | Weak - has known vulnerabilities. Do not use. |
| WPA2 (AES/CCMP) | Current baseline standard. Use if WPA3 unavailable. |
| WPA3 (SAE) | Latest standard. Strongest protection. Use when possible. |
| SSL | Deprecated. Replaced by TLS. Do not use. |
| TLS 1.2 / 1.3 | Current standard for encrypting data in transit. TLS 1.3 is preferred. |
| BitLocker | Windows full-disk encryption. Requires TPM. Pro/Enterprise only. |
| FileVault | macOS full-disk encryption. Built into all macOS versions. |

### \
🦠 Malware Mnemonic

**“Very Wild Tigers Sneak Rapidly Around Forests, Keeping trouble.”**

Each word maps to a malware type:

- **Very** → Virus

- **Wild** → Worm

- **Tigers** → Trojan

- **Sneak** → Spyware

- **Rapidly** → Ransomware

- **Around** → Adware

- **Forests** → Fileless Malware

- **Keeping** → Keylogger

- **Trouble** → Rootkit

The bottom panel illustrates many-to-one PAT, showing multiple internal private IP addresses sharing a single public IP address by appending unique source port numbers to differentiate the traffic rails." />

### 🎣 Social Engineering Mnemonic

**“People Very Smart Will Quickly Identify Tricky Pretexts.”**

Each word maps to an attack type:

- **People** → Phishing (general fraudulent messages)

- **Very** → Vishing (voice‑based phishing)

- **Smart** → Smishing (SMS/text phishing)

- **Will** → Whaling (executives targeted)

- **Quickly** → Quishing (QR code phishing)

- **Identify** → Impersonation (pretending to be trusted staff/vendor)

- **Tricky** → Tailgating/Piggybacking (physical access by following)

- **Pretexts** → Pretexting (fabricated scenario to gain info)

### The

### 📁 File Transfer

**Memory hook:** *“21 to connect, 20 to send” - control channel first,
then data. SFTP rides SSH so it uses port 22.*

| Port | Protocol | TCP/UDP | Description |
|----|----|----|----|
| 20 / 21 | FTP | TCP | File Transfer Protocol. Port 21 = control channel (commands). Port 20 = data channel (file transfer). Unencrypted — avoid on public networks. |
| 22 | SFTP | TCP | SSH File Transfer Protocol. Secure file transfer over SSH tunnel. Replaces FTP. Same port as SSH. |

Quirky Memory Hooks:

- **21** keys to open the **Control** tower, **20** trucks🚚 to move the
  actual **Data.**

- Since **SFTP** relies completely on **SSH**, it steals SSH’s **s**eat
  and rides **s**hotgun on **Port 22**.

### 🔐 Secure Access

**Memory hook:** *“22 is always secure, 23 is never secure” - one number
apart, opposite security*

| Port | Protocol | TCP/UDP | Description |
|----|----|----|----|
| 22 | SSH | TCP | Secure Shell. Encrypted command-line remote access to Linux/Unix servers and network devices. Replaces Telnet. |
| 23 | Telnet | TCP | Unencrypted remote access. Completely obsolete - passwords sent in plain text. Never use on production systems. |

Quirky Memory Hooks:

- Port **22 SSH** is a **S**uper **S**ecure **H**aven.

- Port **23** is a **Tel**-net (**Te**ll-it-to-the-internet) with zero
  safety nets. One number apart, completely opposite worlds.

### 🔧 Network Services

**Memory hook:** *“53 names things, 67/68 assigns addresses, 123 keeps
time”*

| Port | Protocol | TCP/UDP | Description |
|----|----|----|----|
| 53 | DNS | TCP/UDP | Domain Name System. Translates domain names (comptia.org) to IP addresses. UDP for queries, TCP for zone transfers. |
| 67 / 68 | DHCP | UDP | Dynamic Host Configuration Protocol. Port 67 = server. Port 68 = client. Automatically assigns IP addresses. APIPA (169.254.x.x) = DHCP failure. |
| 123 | NTP | UDP | Network Time Protocol. Synchronizes system clocks across devices. Wrong clock = all SSL certificates appear invalid. |
| 161 / 162 | SNMP | UDP | Simple Network Management Protocol. Port 161 = queries to devices. Port 162 = trap alerts from devices. Used for network monitoring. |
| 5060 / 5061 | SIP | TCP/UDP | Session Initiation Protocol. Sets up and manages VoIP calls. Port 5060 = plain. Port 5061 = encrypted TLS version. |

Quirky Memory Hooks:

- Port 53 DNS: A 53 year old **D**igital **N**ame **S**pellbook
  (translates names to numbers).🧙‍♂️

- 67/68 DHCP: Think of an old-school diner where the **(67)** server
  **d**ishes to **h**ungry **c**lients.

- 123: *Tick-tock-tick-tock* (1-2-3**, clock time**!).

- Port 161/162 **SNMP**: The **S**py **N**etwork **M**anagement
  **P**rotocol. Agent **161 asks the questions**, Agent **162 sets the
  trap.**

- 5060/5061: **S**ets **I**nbound **P**hones on your **network lines**.

### 🌐 Network Types Mnemonic

**“Please Let Wireless Make Wide Waves.”**

Each word maps to a network type:

- **Please** → PAN (Personal Area Network) — Bluetooth headset,
  smartwatch.

- **Let** → LAN (Local Area Network) — Home/office wired network.

- **Wireless** → WLAN (Wireless LAN) — Wi‑Fi version of LAN.

- **Make** → MAN (Metropolitan Area Network) — City‑wide, ISP‑managed.

- **Wide** → WAN (Wide Area Network) — Large geographic distances, the
  internet.

- **Waves** → WWAN (Wireless WAN) — Cellular towers, mobile broadband.

### 🖥️ Remote Access

**Memory hook:** *“3389 = Windows Remote Desktop, 5900 = cross-platform
VNC” - always protect both behind a VPN*

| Port | Protocol | TCP/UDP | Description |
|----|----|----|----|
| 3389 | RDP | TCP | Remote Desktop Protocol. Windows graphical remote desktop. Full desktop experience. NEVER expose directly to internet - always use VPN. |
| 5900 | VNC | TCP | Virtual Network Computing. Cross-platform screen sharing. Tools: TightVNC, RealVNC. Always protect behind VPN or SSH tunnel. |

Quirky Memory Hooks:

- **3389:** Three-three-eight-nine, Remote Desktop on the line!

- **5900 (VNC): V**ery **N**osey **C**olleagues have 59 ways to spy on
  your 00 (eyeballs/screen)

### 📂 Directory & File Sharing

**Memory hook:** *“389 looks up people (Active Directory), 445 shares
files (Windows), 636 does 389 securely”*

| Port | Protocol | TCP/UDP | Description |
|----|----|----|----|
| 389 | LDAP | TCP/UDP | Lightweight Directory Access Protocol. Queries Active Directory for user accounts and group memberships. Unencrypted. |
| 636 | LDAPS | TCP | LDAP over SSL. Encrypted version of LDAP. Use instead of port 389 wherever possible. |
| 445 | SMB | TCP | Server Message Block. Windows file and printer sharing. Common ransomware attack vector - keep patched and firewalled. |

Quirky Memory Hooks:

- **389:** “Three-eight-nine, Active Directory is on the line!”

- **636:** “Six-three-six, secures the directory mix!”

- **445:** “Four-four-five, Windows file shares come alive!”

### \

### Number Patterns to Memorize

| Pattern | Ports |
|----|----|
| Always 22 | SSH and SFTP - if it’s secure remote access or secure file transfer, it’s port 22 |
| Sequential pairs | 20/21 = FTP (control/data) 67/68 = DHCP (server/client) |
| Plain → encrypted | 110 → 995 (POP3) 143 → 993 (IMAP) 389 → 636 (LDAP) 80 → 443 (HTTP) 5060 → 5061 (SIP) |
| Four digits | 3389 = RDP 5060/5061 = SIP 5900 = VNC - the only four-digit ports you need |
| UDP only | 67/68 DHCP 123 NTP 161/162 SNMP - fast, fire-and-forget services use UDP |

### ☁️ Cloud Service Models

| Model | Memory Hook |
|----|----|
| SaaS — Software as a Service | “You use it - they run it.” Like a Coffee Shop - you just show up and drink . Examples: Gmail, Microsoft 365, Salesforce. |
| PaaS — Platform as a Service | “You build on it - they manage the platform.” Like a Pottery class - they give you the wheel, you make the pot. Examples: Azure App Service, Google App Engine |
| IaaS — Infrastructure as a Service | “You control the VMs - they own the hardware.” Like IKEA - you get the parts, you build it yourself. Examples: AWS EC2, Azure VMs. |

### 🔌 Must-Know Port Numbers

| Port  | Protocol   | Transport | Description                                  |
|-------|------------|-----------|----------------------------------------------|
| 20/21 | FTP        | TCP       | File Transfer Protocol - 21 control, 20 data |
| 22    | SSH / SFTP | TCP       | Secure Shell - encrypted command line        |
| 23    | Telnet     | TCP       | Unencrypted remote access - obsolete         |
| 25    | SMTP       | TCP       | Send mail outbound                           |
| 53    | DNS        | TCP/UDP   | Domain Name System - name to IP              |
| 67/68 | DHCP       | UDP       | IP address assignment - server/client        |
| 80    | HTTP       | TCP       | Web browsing - unencrypted                   |
| 110   | POP3       | TCP       | Download email to one device                 |
| 143   | IMAP       | TCP       | Sync email across devices                    |
| 389   | LDAP       | TCP/UDP   | Active Directory queries                     |
| 443   | HTTPS      | TCP       | Secure web browsing                          |
| 445   | SMB        | TCP       | Windows file sharing                         |
| 636   | LDAPS      | TCP       | Encrypted LDAP                               |
| 993   | IMAPS      | TCP       | Encrypted IMAP                               |
| 995   | POP3S      | TCP       | Encrypted POP3                               |
| 3389  | RDP        | TCP       | Remote Desktop Protocol                      |

| Cat 5e | 1 Gbps | 100 meters | Minimum requirement for Gigabit networks. |
| Cat 6 | 10 Gbps | 55 meters | Drops to 1 Gbps at full 100m distance and 10 Gbps at up to 55m |
| Cat 6a | 10 Gbps | 100 meters | “a” stands for augmented. Handles full distance. |
| Cat 8 | 40 Gbps | 30 meters | Used primarily in data centers for short server runs. |

### 💻 Command-Line Rosetta Stone (Windows vs. Linux)

| Action                 | Windows Command      | Linux / macOS Command |
|------------------------|----------------------|-----------------------|
| View IP Address        | ipconfig             | ifconfig or ip a      |
| Trace Network Route    | tracert              | traceroute            |
| Test Connectivity      | ping                 | ping                  |
| View Running Processes | tasklist             | ps or top             |
| Kill a Process         | taskkill             | kill                  |
| Elevate Permissions    | Run as Administrator | sudo                  |

### 🪟 Essential Windows Run Commands Quick Reference

Launch these tools directly from the Run dialog (Win + R) or Command
Prompt

| Command | Tool It Opens | What It’s Used For |
|----|----|----|
| compmgmt.msc | Computer Management | The master console. Accesses Event Viewer, Task Scheduler, and local users/groups all in one place. |
| devmgmt.msc | Device Manager | Used to update, roll back, or disable hardware drivers. Look here for the dreaded “yellow exclamation mark.” |
| diskmgmt.msc | Disk Management | Used to initialize new hard drives, format partitions, and assign drive letters. |
| certmgr.msc | Certificate Manager | Manages digital certificates for the current user/computer — encryption, code signing, authentication. |
| lusrmgr.msc | Local Users and Groups | Manages local (non-domain) user accounts and group memberships. Not available on Home editions. |
| gpedit.msc | Local Group Policy Editor | Configures local Group Policy on a single machine. Requires Pro or higher. |
| services.msc | Services | Used to start, stop, or restart background Windows services (like the Print Spooler). |
| mstsc | Remote Desktop Connection | Think “Microsoft Terminal Services Client.” Opens the RDP window to connect to another PC. |
| msinfo32 | System Information | Displays a massive, read-only list of hardware resources, components, and software environments. |
| dxdiag | DirectX Diagnostic Tool | Used to test and verify audio and video hardware functionality. |
| regedit | Registry Editor | The central database for Windows settings. |

> **⚠️ Exam Trap:** Never edit the registry without backing it up first!
>
> **✔ Pro Tip:** If a command manages a specific system component
> through a graphical console, it almost always ends in .msc (Microsoft
> Management Console). If it opens a standalone tool, it usually just
> requires the tool’s name (like cmd, taskmgr, or mstsc)

### 🐧 Essential Linux Commands Quick Reference

| Command | Tool/Action | What It’s Used For |
|----|----|----|
| ls | List files | View directory contents. Equivalent to dir in Windows. |
| pwd | Print working directory | Shows current directory path. |
| cd | Change directory | Navigate between folders. |
| sudo | Superuser do | Run commands with elevated privileges. Equivalent to “Run as Administrator.” |
| chmod | Change mode | Modify file/folder permissions. |
| chown | Change ownership | Assign ownership of files/folders. |
| ps / top | Process status | View running processes. Equivalent to tasklist. |
| kill | Kill process | Terminate a process. Equivalent to taskkill. |
| ifconfig / ip a | Network config | View IP address and network settings. Equivalent to ipconfig. |
| traceroute | Trace route | Diagnose network path issues. Equivalent to tracert. |
| apt-get / yum / dnf | Package managers | Install/remove software packages. Equivalent to Windows Installer. |
| systemd | Init system | Modern Linux service manager — boots the system, starts services, manages system state. |
| /etc/resolv.conf | Config file | Specifies which DNS servers the system uses for name resolution. |
| /etc/fstab | Config file | Lists devices/partitions to auto-mount at boot, with mount points and options. |

### 🍎 Essential macOS Commands & Tools (Quick Reference)

| Command | What It’s Used For |
|----|----|
| Finder | File manager for navigation/storage. |
| System Settings | Configure network, users, displays, privacy |
| Terminal | Command-Line Interface (CLI) for advanced tasks. |
| diskutil | Partition/format drives (CLI). |
| open | Launch apps/files from Terminal. |
| Activity Monitor | CPU, memory, disk, network usage. |
| Time Machine | Built‑in backup with snapshots |
| Mission Control | Overview of all open windows, desktops (Spaces), and full-screen apps for quick navigation. |
| Rapid Security Response (RSR) | Apple's mechanism for delivering critical patches outside the normal full OS update cycle |

alt="A comparative infographic illustrating the structural differences between Transmission Control Protocol (TCP) and User Datagram Protocol (UDP). The left panel depicts TCP as a reliable, connection-oriented protocol, showcasing the three-way handshake process (SYN, SYN-ACK, ACK) and error-checking mechanisms that ensure data delivery. The right panel depicts UDP as a fast, connectionless protocol, showing a continuous stream of data packets sent without a handshake or delivery verification, emphasizing speed over reliability." />

### 💾 Backup Types: Full, Incremental, Differential

- Full = Everything. Slowest to create, fastest to restore.

- Incremental = Changes since LAST backup. Fastest to create, slowest to
  restore (need all incrementals).

- Differential = Changes since LAST FULL. Medium both ways. Need last
  full + latest diff only.
